The rest is parallel. Each body's inertia is turned by the body's orientation and moved to its tree's centre of mass, one invocation per world and body; each dof's motion axis comes from one function shared with the CPU engine, one invocation per world and dof:

dofMotionAxis
/// THE MOTION AXIS OF ONE DOF - its `dof_axes`: the spatial motion a unit velocity of the dof produces, in the frame of
/// its tree's centre of mass. Dof `index_in_joint` of a joint of `joint_type`:
///
///     slide     along the joint's axis                      a pure translation: no angular part, no anchor
///     hinge     about the joint's axis                      (`dofAboutAxis`, the frame's origin `offset` from it)
///     ball      about the body's own axis `index_in_joint`  the CHILD frame's axes - the columns of the body's world
///                                                           rotation - where a ball joint's velocity is defined, and
///                                                           on whose side `integratePos` composes its increment
///                                                           (`rotateQuatByAngularVel`)
///     free      first along the world's three axes, then about the body's own three, exactly as a ball
///
/// `joint_axis` is the joint's axis in the world, `body_rotation` its body's orientation, `offset` the frame's origin
/// minus the joint's anchor. Shared with robotgpu's dof-axis kernel.
pub fn dofMotionAxis(
    joint_type: JointType,
    index_in_joint: u32,
    joint_axis: Vec,
    body_rotation: Quat,
    offset: Vec,
) Motion {
    return switch (joint_type) {
        .slide => .{ .ang = vec_zero, .lin = joint_axis },
        .hinge => dofAboutAxis(joint_axis, offset),
        .ball => dofAboutAxis(rotate(body_rotation, worldAxisAt(index_in_joint)), offset),
        .free => if (index_in_joint < 3)
            .{ .ang = vec_zero, .lin = worldAxisAt(index_in_joint) }
        else
            dofAboutAxis(rotate(body_rotation, worldAxisAt(index_in_joint - 3)), offset),
    };
}

How close the device comes

The device's arithmetic rounds differently from the CPU's in the last bit - a GPU's division and square root are allowed a few units in the last place - so the device is compared with the twin, not required to equal it. The difference is measured against the size of the quantity it belongs to: a vector, a motion or an inertia as a whole, the mass matrix world by world. An error that is small beside an inertia's large diagonal is small, even where it falls on a component close to zero:

   an inertia      [ 52.1   0.0004   -0.0002   12.3 ... ]       its scale: 52.1
   the gap         |device - twin| / (epsilon * scale)            in epsilons, the largest over every quantity

Measured this way, every array of the position stage lies within a few epsilons of the twin, and each is held to a bar of 256 epsilons - room for a phone's GPU, while a kernel that computed the wrong thing would miss by thousands.

48The mass matrix on many worlds

The composite-rigid-body algorithm (§14) builds the mass matrix in two passes: each body's composite inertia, the inertia of its whole subtree moving as one rigid body; then each entry, as the force one dof's unit acceleration demands of that subtree, felt along another dof's axis. On the GPU both passes run for every world at once.

Composite inertias in the CPU's order

A composite inertia is a sum over a subtree, like the centre of mass of §47, and is formed the same way: one invocation per world adds each body's inertia into its parent's, the last body first, so the twin and the CPU engine agree to the bit. A tree's root takes nothing from the world body, which holds it by no rigid joint.

One row per invocation

Row i of the mass matrix has an entry only for the dofs on i's chain of ancestors - a dof far down one leg has nothing to do with a dof on the other leg - so the row is stored sparse, the root's dof first and the diagonal last. One invocation per world and dof fills one row: the force its unit acceleration demands, then that force along each ancestor's axis, walking up from the dof; the armature, the rotor inertia a gearbox adds, joins the diagonal:

   dof i's chain:      root dof  ...  parent's dof  |  i
   row i's slots:      [ M[i][root]  ...  M[i][parent] | M[i][i] + armature[i] ]

   unit force          f   = composite inertia of i's body  x  i's axis       dofUnitForce
   an entry            M[i][j] = f along j's axis                             forceAlongDof

Both steps are robot.zig's own functions, shared by the CPU engine and the kernel:

dofUnitForce
/// THE FORCE ONE DOF'S UNIT ACCELERATION DEMANDS of everything it moves: the composite inertia of the subtree below
/// the dof's body - all of it moves with the dof - times the dof's motion axis. It is one column of the mass matrix, as
/// a spatial force (`forceAlongDof` reads the column's entries off it). Shared with robotgpu's mass-matrix kernel.
pub fn dofUnitForce(composite_inertia: Inertia, dof_motion: Motion) Force {
    return composite_inertia.mul(dof_motion);
}
forceAlongDof
/// THE SHARE OF A SPATIAL FORCE ONE DOF FEELS: the force along the dof's motion axis - the power the force delivers per
/// unit of the dof's velocity. Against `dofUnitForce` of dof i it is the mass matrix's entry M[i][j], for dof j on i's
/// chain of ancestors; against a body's net force it is that dof's generalized force (`rne`). Shared with robotgpu's
/// mass-matrix and RNE kernels.
pub fn forceAlongDof(dof_motion: Motion, force: Force) f32 {
    return dot6(dof_motion, force);
}
mass_matrix_rows
/// ONE ROW OF THE MASS MATRIX, one invocation per world and dof, as robot.zig's `crb` fills it. Dof i's unit
/// acceleration demands a force of its body's composite inertia (`dofUnitForce`); the entry M[i][j], for each dof j on
/// i's chain of ancestors, is that force along j's axis (`forceAlongDof`). The row is stored sparse - only the chain's
/// entries, the root's first and the diagonal last - and the dof's armature, the rotor inertia a gearbox adds, joins
/// the diagonal:
///
///     slots of row i      [ root's dof ... parent's dof | i ]     walked from i up `dof_parent`, filled from the end
///     diagonal            M[i][i] += armature[i]
pub fn mass_matrix_rows(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.dof_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const dof: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const row_start: u32 = tables.mass_row_start.load(dof);
    const row_entries: u32 = tables.mass_row_entry_count.load(dof);
    const body: u32 = tables.dof_body.load(dof);
    const composite: robot.Inertia = state.composite_inertias.loadInertia(world, body);
    const unit_force: robot.Force = robot.dofUnitForce(composite, state.dof_axes.loadMotion(world, dof));
    var slot: u32 = row_entries;
    var ancestor_dof: u32 = dof;
    while (ancestor_dof != robot.no_dof) : (ancestor_dof = tables.dof_parent.load(ancestor_dof)) {
        slot -= 1;
        const entry: f32 = robot.forceAlongDof(state.dof_axes.loadMotion(world, ancestor_dof), unit_force);
        state.mass_matrix.store(world, row_start + slot, entry);
    }
    const diagonal_slot: u32 = row_start + row_entries - 1;
    const diagonal: f32 = state.mass_matrix.load(world, diagonal_slot) + constants.dof_armature.load(dof);
    state.mass_matrix.store(world, diagonal_slot, diagonal);
}

On the device, the composite inertias and the matrix's entries lie within about eight epsilons of the twin's, measured against each quantity's size (§47).

49Factoring and solving on many worlds

With the mass matrix built, a step factors it and solves with it: the accelerations the forces other than the constraints' would cause, M v̇ = f. The factorisation is the sparse LDLT of §15 - along each dof's chain of ancestors, with no fill-in - and the GPU runs the very same code.

One definition over a storage

The factorisation and the solve are written once, over a STORAGE: whatever gives the model's sparsity - each row's start and length, each entry's column - and the factor being built in place, with its inverse pivots; for the solve, also the vector solved in place. The CPU engine's storage is its own arrays; the GPU's is one world's arrays in the arenas. Both engines therefore factor with the same arithmetic in the same order, and the twin matches the CPU engine to the bit:

   factorLdlInPlace(storage, dofs)        solveLdl(storage, dofs)
          |                                     |
   the CPU engine:  its own arrays        a GPU world:  model_u's sparsity, world_f's factor and vector
solveLdl
/// THE SOLVE M x = b WITH THE FACTOR (`factorLdlInPlace`), in place over b in `storage`: L^T backward - each dof's
/// value pushed into its ancestors' - then the inverse pivots, then L forward - each dof's value pulled from its
/// ancestors'. A root dof has no ancestors, and a zero value nothing to push.
pub inline fn solveLdl(storage: anytype, dof_count: u32) void {
    comptime requireMassSolveStorage(@TypeOf(storage));
    var k: u32 = dof_count;
    while (k > 0) {
        k -= 1;
        const row_entries: u32 = storage.rowEntries(k);
        const value: f32 = storage.solution(k);
        if (row_entries == 1 or value == 0.0) {
            continue;
        }
        const start: u32 = storage.rowStart(k);
        var entry: u32 = start;
        while (entry < start + row_entries - 1) : (entry += 1) {
            const ancestor: u32 = storage.column(entry);
            storage.setSolution(ancestor, storage.solution(ancestor) - storage.factor(entry) * value);
        }
    }
    var dof: u32 = 0;
    while (dof < dof_count) : (dof += 1) {
        storage.setSolution(dof, storage.solution(dof) * storage.inversePivot(dof));
    }
    dof = 0;
    while (dof < dof_count) : (dof += 1) {
        const row_entries: u32 = storage.rowEntries(dof);
        if (row_entries == 1) {
            continue;
        }
        const start: u32 = storage.rowStart(dof);
        var pulled: f32 = 0;
        var entry: u32 = start;
        while (entry < start + row_entries - 1) : (entry += 1) {
            pulled += storage.factor(entry) * storage.solution(storage.column(entry));
        }
        storage.setSolution(dof, storage.solution(dof) - pulled);
    }
}
WorldMassMatrix
/// ONE WORLD'S MASS MATRIX as robot.zig's factorisation and solve read it (`robot.factorLdlInPlace`,
/// `robot.solveLdl`): the model's sparsity from `model_u`, and three per-world arrays named at compile time - the
/// factor (`factor_array`) and its inverse pivots (`inverse_diagonal_array`), both in `world_f`, and the vector solved
/// in place (`solution_array`, in the arena `solution_arena`) - so each use names what it factors and what it solves
/// into (`SmoothAccelerationSolve`, `VelocityChangeSolve`, `ConstraintRowSolve`). Global memory - the first form; a
/// storage in workgroup memory would replace it, the arithmetic untouched, should a profile ask.
fn WorldMassMatrix(
    comptime factor_array: [:0]const u8,
    comptime inverse_diagonal_array: [:0]const u8,
    comptime solution_arena: Arena,
    comptime solution_array: [:0]const u8,
) type {
    return struct {
        const Storage = @This();
        /// The layout of the solution's arena: `WorldStateLayout` for `world_f`, `ConstraintRowsLayout` for `efc_f`.
        const SolutionLayout: type = @FieldType(Layout, @tagName(solution_arena));

        world: u32,
        /// The item of the solution's dof 0 in the world's block of `solution_array`: 0 for an array with one number
        /// per dof, `row x dof_count` for one row of a row-major [row][dof] array (`constraint_row_dofs`).
        solution_first: u32 = 0,

        pub fn rowStart(storage: Storage, dof: u32) u32 {
            _ = storage;
            return params.layout.model_u.mass_row_start.load(dof);
        }
        pub fn rowEntries(storage: Storage, dof: u32) u32 {
            _ = storage;
            return params.layout.model_u.mass_row_entry_count.load(dof);
        }
        pub fn column(storage: Storage, entry: u32) u32 {
            _ = storage;
            return params.layout.model_u.mass_entry_column.load(entry);
        }
        pub fn factor(storage: Storage, entry: u32) f32 {
            return @field(params.layout.world_f, factor_array).load(storage.world, entry);
        }
        pub fn setFactor(storage: Storage, entry: u32, value: f32) void {
            @field(params.layout.world_f, factor_array).store(storage.world, entry, value);
        }
        pub fn inversePivot(storage: Storage, dof: u32) f32 {
            return @field(params.layout.world_f, inverse_diagonal_array).load(storage.world, dof);
        }
        pub fn setInversePivot(storage: Storage, dof: u32, value: f32) void {
            @field(params.layout.world_f, inverse_diagonal_array).store(storage.world, dof, value);
        }
        pub fn solution(storage: Storage, dof: u32) f32 {
            const item: u32 = storage.solution_first + dof;
            const solution_layout: SolutionLayout = @field(params.layout, @tagName(solution_arena));
            return @field(solution_layout, solution_array).load(storage.world, item);
        }
        pub fn setSolution(storage: Storage, dof: u32, value: f32) void {
            const item: u32 = storage.solution_first + dof;
            const solution_layout: SolutionLayout = @field(params.layout, @tagName(solution_arena));
            @field(solution_layout, solution_array).store(storage.world, item, value);
        }
    };
}

One invocation per world

Each world's factorisation is a chain of eliminations, each depending on the ones before, so a world is factored by one invocation, from the leaves inward; the worlds run side by side. The world's mass matrix is copied into its factor, and factored in place; the solve copies the forces into the acceleration and solves there.

factor_mass_matrix
/// THE MASS MATRIX FACTORED, one invocation per world: the world's M copied into its factor, then factored in place by
/// robot.zig's own `factorLdlInPlace` - the CPU engine's arithmetic in its order, so the twin matches it to the bit.
/// One invocation per world is the first form, the exact one; the storage decides where the factor lives.
pub fn factor_mass_matrix(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    var entry: u32 = 0;
    while (entry < params.counts.mass_entry_count) : (entry += 1) {
        state.mass_factor.store(world, entry, state.mass_matrix.load(world, entry));
    }
    const storage: SmoothAccelerationSolve = .{ .world = world };
    robot.factorLdlInPlace(storage, params.counts.dof_count);
}

Why the factor comes less close

On the device, the mass matrix lies within about two epsilons of the twin's, its factor and the acceleration within about eighty. A factorisation carries the matrix's CONDITIONING: a small error in the matrix can grow, in the factor and the solution, by up to the matrix's condition number - and a robot's mass matrix joins heavy links to light ones, so it is far from perfectly conditioned. The factor, its pivots and the acceleration are held to a bar of 1024 epsilons, the rest of the step to 256.

50Velocities on many worlds

Velocities accumulate down the tree exactly as poses do: a body moves with its parent, plus whatever its own joints add (§15). So the GPU computes them the way it poses the bodies (§46) - one invocation per world and branch, each walking its branch from the root down to its leaf, a body on several branches given the same bits by each.

For each body, the walk starts from the parent's velocity and carries it through the body's joints one at a time with the CPU engine's own function, velocityThroughJoint, which also records how fast each dof's axis is being dragged - the ingredient of the centrifugal and Coriolis forces. A body whose parent is the world starts from rest:

   branch:   pelvis -> thigh -> shin -> foot

   pelvis    v = 0               then through its free joint      (translations, then rotations)
   thigh     v = v(pelvis)       then through its ball joint      (three rotations, one snapshot)
   shin      v = v(thigh)        then through its joints
   foot      v = v(shin)         then through its joints
body_velocities_branch
/// EVERY BODY'S VELOCITY, and the rate each dof's motion axis is dragged at, one invocation per world and branch -
/// MuJoCo Warp's `_comvel_branch`, in robot.zig's arithmetic (`comVel`). Velocities accumulate down the tree as poses
/// do, so an invocation walks its branch from the root down to its leaf: a body's velocity is its parent's, carried
/// through each of its joints in turn (`robot.velocityThroughJoint`), and a body on several branches gets the same bits
/// from each. A body whose parent is the world starts from rest directly - the very value robot.zig reads for body 0 -
/// so no branch reads the world body's velocity while another writes it; each world's first branch writes it, zero.
pub fn body_velocities_branch(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.branch_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const branch: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const at_rest: robot.Motion = .zero;
    if (branch == 0) {
        state.body_velocities.storeMotion(world, 0, at_rest);
    }
    const storage: WorldVelocities = .{ .world = world };
    const first_entry: u32 = tables.body_branch_start.load(branch);
    const end_entry: u32 = tables.body_branch_start.load(branch + 1);
    var entry: u32 = first_entry;
    while (entry < end_entry) : (entry += 1) {
        const body: u32 = tables.body_branches.load(entry);
        const parent: u32 = tables.body_parent.load(body);
        var velocity: robot.Motion = if (parent == 0) at_rest else state.body_velocities.loadMotion(world, parent);
        const first_joint: u32 = tables.body_first_joint.load(body);
        const end_joint: u32 = first_joint + tables.body_joint_count.load(body);
        var joint: u32 = first_joint;
        while (joint < end_joint) : (joint += 1) {
            const joint_type: robot.JointType = typeOfJoint(joint);
            const first_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
            velocity = robot.velocityThroughJoint(storage, joint_type, first_dof, velocity);
        }
        state.body_velocities.storeMotion(world, body, velocity);
    }
}

On the device, every body's velocity lies within about five epsilons of the twin's, and every axis rate within about fifteen, against the step's bar of 256.

51Bias forces on many worlds

The bias forces c(q,v) - weight, and the centrifugal and Coriolis forces of moving bodies (§16) - come from the recursive Newton-Euler algorithm run with no joint accelerating. On the GPU its four passes become four kernels, each in the shape its pass needs:

   forward     each body's acceleration       per world and branch    flows down the tree, as velocities do
   per body    each body's own force           per world and body      f = I a + v x* (I v), independent
   backward    each subtree's force            per world               summed from the leaves, the CPU's order
   per dof     each dof's bias force           per world and dof       the subtree's force along the dof's axis

Gravity enters the forward pass as an upward acceleration of the world, of size g: in a frame accelerating upward at g, every body's weight appears by itself, with no body treated specially. A body hanging from the world starts its acceleration there; every other body starts from its parent's and adds the drag of its own moving axes.

bias_accelerations_branch
/// EVERY BODY'S ACCELERATION WITH NO JOINT ACCELERATING, one invocation per world and branch - MuJoCo Warp's
/// `_cacc_branch`, in robot.zig's arithmetic (`rne`). An invocation walks its branch from the root down: a body's
/// acceleration is its parent's, plus each of its dofs' axis rate times the dof's rate, in dof order - even at a
/// constant joint rate, an axis that turns accelerates whatever it drives. A body whose parent is the world starts
/// from the world's acceleration, upward at g, directly - the value robot.zig reads for body 0 - so no branch reads
/// the world body's acceleration while another writes it; each world's first branch writes it.
pub fn bias_accelerations_branch(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.branch_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const branch: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const gravity: Vec = params.options.gravityVector();
    const world_acceleration: robot.Motion = .{ .ang = zm.vec_zero, .lin = -gravity };
    if (branch == 0) {
        state.body_bias_accelerations.storeMotion(world, 0, world_acceleration);
    }
    const first_entry: u32 = tables.body_branch_start.load(branch);
    const end_entry: u32 = tables.body_branch_start.load(branch + 1);
    var entry: u32 = first_entry;
    while (entry < end_entry) : (entry += 1) {
        const body: u32 = tables.body_branches.load(entry);
        const parent: u32 = tables.body_parent.load(body);
        var acceleration: robot.Motion = if (parent == 0)
            world_acceleration
        else
            state.body_bias_accelerations.loadMotion(world, parent);
        const first_dof: u32 = tables.body_first_dof.load(body);
        const end_dof: u32 = first_dof + tables.body_dof_count.load(body);
        var dof: u32 = first_dof;
        while (dof < end_dof) : (dof += 1) {
            const axis_rate: robot.Motion = state.dof_axis_rates.loadMotion(world, dof);
            const joint_rate: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, dof);
            acceleration = acceleration.add(axis_rate.scale(joint_rate));
        }
        state.body_bias_accelerations.storeMotion(world, body, acceleration);
    }
}

Each body's own force is Newton-Euler for one rigid body, computed by the very function the CPU engine uses; the backward sum then turns it into the force the body's whole subtree needs, which is what its joint must carry:

body_bias_forces
/// EACH BODY'S OWN FORCE, one invocation per world and body: Newton-Euler for a rigid body in spatial form,
/// `f = I a + v x* (I v)` (`robot.bodyNewtonEulerForce`) - the force that gives the body its acceleration, plus the
/// gyroscopic and centrifugal force its own motion carries. The world body needs none; its force is zero.
pub fn body_bias_forces(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.body_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const body: u32 = handled.element;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    if (body == 0) {
        state.subtree_bias_forces.storeForce(world, 0, .zero);
        return;
    }
    const inertia: robot.Inertia = state.body_inertias.loadInertia(world, body);
    const acceleration: robot.Motion = state.body_bias_accelerations.loadMotion(world, body);
    const velocity: robot.Motion = state.body_velocities.loadMotion(world, body);
    const force: robot.Force = robot.bodyNewtonEulerForce(inertia, acceleration, velocity);
    state.subtree_bias_forces.storeForce(world, body, force);
}

On the device, the accelerations lie within about thirteen epsilons of the twin's, the subtree forces within about twenty-three, and the bias forces within about four, against the step's bar of 256.

52Passive forces on many worlds

A joint can carry its own spring and damper - forces that come from the mechanism, not from any motor. A damper opposes motion: on each of the joint's dofs it pushes back in proportion to the dof's rate. A spring pulls a hinge or a slide back toward its reference position, in proportion to the distance (§17). Ball and free joints carry no spring here.

No sum crosses dofs: each dof's passive force depends only on its own joint, so the GPU gives every world and dof an invocation of its own. Each follows the CPU engine's order exactly - the damper's term first, then the spring's, each only when its coefficient is not zero:

   passive force  =  0
                  -  damping   x  rate                         when damping is not zero
                  -  stiffness x  (position - reference)       a hinge or slide, when stiffness is not zero
passive_joint_forces
/// EACH DOF'S PASSIVE FORCE, one invocation per world and dof - robot.zig's `passive` for a dof (tendons are refused
/// before packing, `refuseUnsimulatedFeatures`): zero, less its joint's damping times the dof's rate, less - for a
/// hinge or a slide, whose one dof this is - its stiffness times the coordinate's distance from its reference. MJW
/// computes it per joint (`_spring_damper_dof_passive`); per dof keeps robot.zig's order without any dof waiting for
/// another.
pub fn passive_joint_forces(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.dof_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const dof: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const joint: u32 = tables.dof_joint.load(dof);
    var passive_force: f32 = 0.0;
    // The damper: on every dof of its joint, against the dof's rate.
    const damping: f32 = constants.joint_damping.load(joint);
    if (damping != 0.0) {
        const rate: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, dof);
        passive_force -= damping * rate;
    }
    // The spring: on a hinge's or a slide's one dof, toward the reference position. The type is read only for a joint
    // that has a spring at all, as robot.zig reads it.
    const stiffness: f32 = constants.joint_stiffness.load(joint);
    if (stiffness != 0.0) {
        const joint_type: robot.JointType = typeOfJoint(joint);
        if (joint_type == .hinge or joint_type == .slide) {
            const first_coordinate: u32 = tables.joint_first_coordinate.load(joint);
            const joint_position: f32 = state.joint_positions.load(world, first_coordinate);
            const spring_reference: f32 = constants.joint_reference_position.load(joint);
            passive_force -= stiffness * (joint_position - spring_reference);
        }
    }
    state.passive_joint_forces.store(world, dof, passive_force);
}

A model with tendons is refused before anything is packed: a tendon's spring acts on its length, which needs kernels of its own, and the device runs no model with part of its physics silently missing.

53Actuation on many worlds

An actuator turns a control - the number a policy or a controller sets each step - into a force on a joint (§18). Three steps, each the CPU engine's: the control is clamped to the actuator's range; the activation, an internal state some actuators carry, has its rate written (none, an integrator, or a filter chasing the control); and the force follows an affine law in the activation, the actuator's length and its velocity, clamped in turn:

   force  =  gain x input  +  bias0  +  bias1 x length  +  bias2 x velocity

   input  =  the control                 no activation
             the activation's value       an integrator or a filter, whose rate is written for the advance

Every actuator is independent, so each world and actuator gets an invocation of its own:

actuator_forces
/// EACH ACTUATOR'S FORCE, one invocation per world and actuator - robot.zig's `actuation` for one actuator, after its
/// `transmission` (MJW: `transmission`, `_actuator_velocity`, `_actuator_force`). The length and velocity, the clamped
/// control, the activation's rate and the force are each written for the stages after.
pub fn actuator_forces(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.actuator_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const actuator: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    // The transmission: a joint actuator's actuator_length and actuator_velocity are its joint's coordinate and rate.
    const joint: u32 = tables.actuator_target.load(actuator);
    const first_coordinate: u32 = tables.joint_first_coordinate.load(joint);
    const first_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
    const actuator_length: f32 = state.joint_positions.load(world, first_coordinate);
    const actuator_velocity: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, first_dof);
    state.actuator_lengths.store(world, actuator, actuator_length);
    state.actuator_velocities.store(world, actuator, actuator_velocity);
    // The control, clamped to its range when it has one.
    const commanded: f32 = state.controls.load(world, actuator);
    const control_limited: bool = tables.actuator_control_limited.load(actuator) != 0;
    const control: f32 = if (control_limited) clamped: {
        const control_range: [2]f32 = constants.actuator_control_range.loadPair(actuator);
        break :clamped clamp(commanded, control_range[0], control_range[1]);
    } else commanded;
    // The activation: its rate written, its current value the force's input - or the control itself, for none.
    const activation_index: u32 = tables.actuator_activation_index.load(actuator);
    const dynamics: ActivationDynamics = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(tables.actuator_dynamics.load(actuator)));
    const input: f32 = switch (dynamics) {
        .none => control,
        .integrator => integrated: {
            state.activation_rates.store(world, activation_index, control);
            break :integrated state.activations.load(world, activation_index);
        },
        .filter => filtered: {
            const activation: f32 = state.activations.load(world, activation_index);
            const time_constant: f32 = constants.actuator_time_constant.load(actuator);
            const rate: f32 = (control - activation) / time_constant;
            state.activation_rates.store(world, activation_index, rate);
            break :filtered activation;
        },
        .filter_exact => filtered_exactly: {
            // The exact update `w += (u - w)(1 - e^(-dt/tau))`, stable for any time constant, reported as the
            // equivalent rate so the advance integrates every activation alike.
            const activation: f32 = state.activations.load(world, activation_index);
            const time_constant: f32 = constants.actuator_time_constant.load(actuator);
            const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
            const alpha: f32 = 1.0 - @exp(-timestep / time_constant);
            const rate: f32 = (control - activation) * alpha / timestep;
            state.activation_rates.store(world, activation_index, rate);
            break :filtered_exactly activation;
        },
    };
    // The force: affine in the input, the length and the velocity, then clamped to its range when it has one.
    const gain: f32 = constants.actuator_gain.load(actuator);
    const bias: Vec = constants.actuator_bias.loadVec(actuator);
    const affine_force: f32 = gain * input + bias[0] + bias[1] * actuator_length + bias[2] * actuator_velocity;
    const force_limited: bool = tables.actuator_force_limited.load(actuator) != 0;
    const force: f32 = if (force_limited) clamped: {
        const force_range: [2]f32 = constants.actuator_force_range.loadPair(actuator);
        break :clamped clamp(affine_force, force_range[0], force_range[1]);
    } else affine_force;
    state.actuator_forces.store(world, actuator, force);
}

A gather instead of a scatter

A joint actuator's force lands on its joint's one dof. MuJoCo Warp scatters each actuator's force onto its dof with an atomic add, whose order depends on scheduling. Here each dof gathers instead: the model is packed with each dof's list of the actuators acting on it, in actuator order, and one invocation per world and dof adds them up from zero - the CPU engine's sum, to the bit, and the same on every run:

   dof 7's actuators:   [ 12, 19 ]          (packed with the model, in actuator order)
   joint force[7]   =   0 + force[12] + force[19]
actuator_joint_forces
/// EACH DOF'S ACTUATOR FORCE, one invocation per world and dof: the forces of the joint actuators acting on the dof,
/// added in actuator order (`dof_actuators`) from zero - robot.zig's `+=` into a cleared array, to the bit, where
/// MJW's `_qfrc_actuator` scatters with an atomic add.
pub fn actuator_joint_forces(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.dof_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const dof: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const first_entry: u32 = tables.dof_first_actuator.load(dof);
    const end_entry: u32 = first_entry + tables.dof_actuator_count.load(dof);
    var joint_force: f32 = 0.0;
    var entry: u32 = first_entry;
    while (entry < end_entry) : (entry += 1) {
        const actuator: u32 = tables.dof_actuators.load(entry);
        joint_force += state.actuator_forces.load(world, actuator);
    }
    state.actuator_joint_forces.store(world, dof, joint_force);
}

54The velocity update on many worlds

A step carries each joint's velocity over the timestep by its acceleration - Euler's update, velocity += dt × acceleration (§19). With no constraint forces, the acceleration is the smooth one of §49.

Damping, taken implicitly

A joint's damper makes that explicit update fragile: a damper stiff enough, against a light body, makes the update overshoot - the velocity grows where it should decay. So a model with damping takes the damper implicitly, at the end of the step: the velocity change dv solves

   (M + dt D) dv  =  dt (M a)          D: each dof's damper on the diagonal

which stays stable however stiff the damper. It is four steps, the CPU engine's, run per world:

   the right-hand side    dt (M a)      the product with the mass matrix, then scaled by dt
   the damped matrix      M + dt D      M copied, dt x damping added to each damped dof's diagonal
   the factor             L^T D L       the same factorisation as M's (§49)
   the solve              dv            in place over the right-hand side

The product reads each stored entry of M twice - once for its own row, once mirrored into an ancestor's row - so the order of its loop is part of its result, and the GPU calls the CPU engine's own function for it, as for the factor and the solve. The damped matrix is factored into arrays of its own, so M's factor stays as it is for anything that solves with M afterwards.

damped_velocity_changes
/// EACH WORLD'S DAMPED VELOCITY CHANGE, one invocation per world - robot.zig's `dampedVelocityStep` for a model with
/// damping, in its four steps (the section's introduction); a model without damping has nothing to do here.
pub fn damped_velocity_changes(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    if (tables.has_dof_damping.load(0) == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
    const dof_count: u32 = params.counts.dof_count;
    // The right-hand side: dt (M a).
    const product: WorldMassProduct = .{ .world = world };
    robot.multiplyByMassMatrix(product, dof_count);
    var scaled_dof: u32 = 0;
    while (scaled_dof < dof_count) : (scaled_dof += 1) {
        const scaled: f32 = state.velocity_changes.load(world, scaled_dof) * timestep;
        state.velocity_changes.store(world, scaled_dof, scaled);
    }
    // The damped matrix: M, with dt x damping on each damped joint's dofs' diagonals.
    var entry: u32 = 0;
    while (entry < params.counts.mass_entry_count) : (entry += 1) {
        state.damped_mass_factor.store(world, entry, state.mass_matrix.load(world, entry));
    }
    var joint: u32 = 0;
    while (joint < params.counts.joint_count) : (joint += 1) {
        const damping: f32 = constants.joint_damping.load(joint);
        if (damping == 0) {
            continue;
        }
        const first_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
        const joint_type: robot.JointType = typeOfJoint(joint);
        const joint_dof_count: u32 = joint_type.dofCount();
        var offset: u32 = 0;
        while (offset < joint_dof_count) : (offset += 1) {
            const damped_dof: u32 = first_dof + offset;
            const row_start: u32 = tables.mass_row_start.load(damped_dof);
            const diagonal: u32 = row_start + tables.mass_row_entry_count.load(damped_dof) - 1;
            const damped: f32 = state.damped_mass_factor.load(world, diagonal) + timestep * damping;
            state.damped_mass_factor.store(world, diagonal, damped);
        }
    }
    // The factor, and the solve in place over the right-hand side.
    const storage: VelocityChangeSolve = .{ .world = world };
    robot.factorLdlInPlace(storage, dof_count);
    robot.solveLdl(storage, dof_count);
}

Each dof then adds its change - or, for a model without damping, dt × acceleration - one invocation per world and dof:

next_velocities
/// EACH DOF'S NEXT VELOCITY, one invocation per world and dof: its velocity plus the damped change, or - a model
/// without damping - plus `dt * acceleration`, as robot.zig adds them.
pub fn next_velocities(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.dof_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const dof: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const velocity: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, dof);
    const has_damping: bool = tables.has_dof_damping.load(0) != 0;
    if (has_damping) {
        // The change solved for the whole world, the damper taken implicitly (`damped_velocity_changes`).
        const change: f32 = state.velocity_changes.load(world, dof);
        state.joint_velocities.store(world, dof, velocity + change);
        return;
    }
    // Without damping, the explicit update.
    const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
    const acceleration: f32 = state.joint_accelerations.load(world, dof);
    state.joint_velocities.store(world, dof, velocity + timestep * acceleration);
}

55The advance on many worlds

After the velocities, the rest of an Euler step carries the state over the timestep (§19), each piece on its own element, so each gets its own invocations:

   the activations     activation += dt x rate                       per world and activation
   the bound           a velocity that is not a number becomes 0,    per world and dof
                       any other is clamped to the model's bound
   the positions       slide, hinge:  q += dt x rate                 per world and joint
                       free:  position += dt x velocity, then the quaternion turned
                       ball:  the quaternion turned
                       every quaternion normalised again

The bound sits between the two integrations on purpose: a velocity gone wrong is caught before it reaches the positions, where it would carry into every later step.

Turning a quaternion

A free or a ball joint's orientation is a quaternion, and its angular velocity turns it by the rotation of angle |w| dt about w's direction - the CPU engine's own function, so the twin turns it to the bit. Each turn rounds, and a quaternion that drifts from unit length stops being a rotation, so every one is normalised again at once - a step that depends only on that quaternion, so it runs in the same invocation:

turnQuaternion
/// The quaternion at coordinates `first_coordinate`..+4 of world `world` turned by the angular velocity at dofs
/// `first_dof`..+3 over `timestep` (`robot.rotateQuatByAngularVel`), then normalised (`robot.normalizeQuat`) - a free
/// or a ball joint's orientation, carried over the step.
fn turnQuaternion(world: u32, first_coordinate: u32, first_dof: u32, timestep: f32) void {
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const angular_velocity: Vec = state.joint_velocities.loadVector3(world, first_dof);
    const quaternion: robot.Quat = state.joint_positions.loadQuaternion(world, first_coordinate);
    const turned: robot.Quat = robot.rotateQuatByAngularVel(quaternion, angular_velocity, timestep);
    const normalised: robot.Quat = robot.normalizeQuat(turned);
    state.joint_positions.storeQuaternion(world, first_coordinate, normalised);
}
next_positions
/// EACH JOINT'S COORDINATES, one invocation per world and joint - robot.zig's `integratePos` for the joint, then its
/// `normalizeQuats` for the joint's quaternion, which depends on nothing but that quaternion (MJW: `_next_position`).
pub fn next_positions(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.joint_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const joint: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
    const first_coordinate: u32 = tables.joint_first_coordinate.load(joint);
    const first_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
    const joint_type: robot.JointType = typeOfJoint(joint);
    switch (joint_type) {
        .slide, .hinge => {
            const position: f32 = state.joint_positions.load(world, first_coordinate);
            const rate: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, first_dof);
            state.joint_positions.store(world, first_coordinate, position + timestep * rate);
        },
        .free => {
            // The position, carried by the linear velocity - each lane rounded as robot.zig rounds each coordinate.
            const position: Vec = state.joint_positions.loadVector3(world, first_coordinate);
            const linear_velocity: Vec = state.joint_velocities.loadVector3(world, first_dof);
            const moved: Vec = position + splat(timestep) * linear_velocity;
            state.joint_positions.storeVector3(world, first_coordinate, moved);
            turnQuaternion(world, first_coordinate + 3, first_dof + 3, timestep);
        },
        .ball => {
            turnQuaternion(world, first_coordinate, first_dof, timestep);
        },
    }
}

56Constraint rows on many worlds

The stages so far move a mechanism freely. A real one meets resistance: a joint reaches the end of its travel, a foot meets the floor. Each such push is a CONSTRAINT ROW - one scalar condition the solver enforces, softly (§30), such as "this joint stays above its lower end". The rows a world has change from step to step, as joints come near their ends and leave them again, so every step begins by finding them.

A joint's two ends

A hinge or a slide with a RANGE [lo, hi] has two ENDS, and each is a CANDIDATE for a row. Each candidate's DISTANCE is how far the joint is from its end, measured toward the inside of the range, and the candidate becomes a row while that distance is below the joint's MARGIN - the distance at which the end starts to push:

          margin                                            margin
       |<------>|                                        |<------>|
   ----+--------+----------------------------------------+--------+----   q
       lo                                                         hi

   lower end:   distance = q - lo      a row while it is below the margin
   upper end:   distance = hi - q      a row while it is below the margin
   either row:  violation = distance - margin      0 where the end starts to push, negative beyond

With a margin of zero, an end becomes a row only once the joint has passed it; with a positive margin, it starts to push a little before. The violation is what the row's softness acts on - the damped spring of its reference acceleration (§20) and its impedance (§30): the deeper inside the margin, the harder it pushes back.

Two passes, in a fixed order

The rows are found in two passes:

   limit_candidates   per world and candidate    the distance to the end; whether it is inside the margin
   constraint_rows    per world                  the active candidates, packed into rows from row 0

Every candidate has a slot fixed by the model - both ends of every limited joint, in joint order, the lower end first, which is the order robot.zig and MuJoCo emit their limit rows in - and one invocation per world packs the active ones. So a world's rows come in the same order however the GPU schedules its invocations. MuJoCo Warp instead hands each new row the next index from an atomic counter, and its rows come in whatever order its threads arrive; the solver's answer then depends on the schedule, if only in the last bits.

An example

A joint with the range [-2.96, 2.96] and a margin of 0.05, in two worlds:

                 q        lower distance    upper distance    rows
   world 0    -2.98       -0.02             5.94              the lower end, violation -0.07
   world 1     2.935       5.895            0.025             the upper end, violation -0.025

In world 0 the joint has passed its lower end by 0.02, so the row's violation is that plus the margin; in world 1 it is still inside its range, but within the margin of its upper end, so that end already pushes - gently, its violation half the margin.

limit_candidates
/// EACH LIMIT CANDIDATE'S DISTANCE TO ITS END, one invocation per world and candidate. Candidate c is an end of joint
/// `limited_joints[c / 2]` - the lower end when c is even, the upper when it is odd - and its distance is how far the
/// joint is from that end, measured toward the inside of the range:
///
///     lower end    distance = q - lo        positive inside the range, negative once q has passed below lo
///     upper end    distance = hi - q        positive inside the range, negative once q has passed above hi
///
/// The candidate is ACTIVE - it will become a row - while its distance is below the joint's margin: the end begins to
/// push before the joint reaches it when the margin is positive, and only once the joint is past it when the margin
/// is zero. robot.zig computes both in exactly this arithmetic.
pub fn limit_candidates(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.limit_candidate_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const candidate: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const joint: u32 = tables.limited_joints.load(candidate / 2);
    const is_upper_end: bool = candidate % 2 == 1;
    const coordinate: u32 = tables.joint_first_coordinate.load(joint);
    const joint_position: f32 = state.joint_positions.load(world, coordinate);
    const range: [2]f32 = constants.joint_range.loadPair(joint);
    const lower_end: f32 = range[0];
    const upper_end: f32 = range[1];
    const distance_to_end: f32 = if (is_upper_end) upper_end - joint_position else joint_position - lower_end;
    const margin: f32 = constants.joint_limit_margin.load(joint);
    // robot.zig's own test, word for word - an end is skipped when its distance is at or beyond the margin - rather
    // than `distance < margin`: the two agree for every number, but not for NaN, where only this form keeps the row.
    // A joint gone NaN then keeps a NaN row, as on the CPU, instead of silently losing its limit.
    const is_beyond_margin: bool = distance_to_end >= margin;
    // A model whose options disable its constraints makes no row (robot.zig makes none: `makeConstraints` is skipped).
    const constraints_enabled: bool = params.options.constraints == .enabled;
    const is_active: bool = constraints_enabled and !is_beyond_margin;
    params.layout.efc_f.limit_candidate_distance.store(world, candidate, distance_to_end);
    params.layout.world_u.limit_candidate_active.store(world, candidate, @intFromBool(is_active));
}
constraint_rows
/// EACH WORLD'S ROWS, one invocation per world: its active candidates, in candidate order, packed into rows from
/// row 0 - each row recording the candidate it came from, its VIOLATION (the candidate's distance minus its joint's
/// margin: zero where the end starts to push, negative the further inside the margin the joint has gone), its
/// Jacobian's one nonzero and - under the reference pose's inertia, MuJoCo's default - its inertia, the joint's
/// inverse weight at the reference pose (MuJoCo's `efc_diagApprox` for a limit). Then the floor's contacts' rows
/// (`packContactRows`), when the worlds have a floor. The world's row count follows. The device's rows hold every
/// candidate and four rows a floor slot (`Counts.constraint_capacity`), so a world never runs out of them.
pub fn constraint_rows(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const integers: WorldIntegersLayout = params.layout.world_u;
    const rows: ConstraintRowsLayout = params.layout.efc_f;
    var rows_packed: u32 = 0;
    var candidate: u32 = 0;
    while (candidate < params.counts.limit_candidate_count) : (candidate += 1) {
        const is_active: bool = integers.limit_candidate_active.load(world, candidate) != 0;
        if (!is_active) {
            continue;
        }
        const joint: u32 = tables.limited_joints.load(candidate / 2);
        const margin: f32 = constants.joint_limit_margin.load(joint);
        const distance_to_end: f32 = rows.limit_candidate_distance.load(world, candidate);
        const violation: f32 = distance_to_end - margin;
        integers.constraint_row_kind.store(world, rows_packed, @backingInt(robot.ConstraintKind.limit));
        integers.constraint_row_source.store(world, rows_packed, candidate);
        rows.constraint_violation.store(world, rows_packed, violation);
        // The row's Jacobian, one nonzero: the distance grows with the joint's velocity at a lower end and shrinks
        // with it at an upper end.
        const is_upper_end: bool = candidate % 2 == 1;
        const jacobian_entry: f32 = if (is_upper_end) -1.0 else 1.0;
        const first_slot: u32 = rows_packed * params.counts.row_nonzero_capacity;
        const joint_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
        integers.constraint_jacobian_nonzero_count.store(world, rows_packed, 1);
        integers.constraint_jacobian_dofs.store(world, first_slot, joint_dof);
        rows.constraint_jacobian_values.store(world, first_slot, jacobian_entry);
        if (params.options.constraint_inertia == .reference_pose) {
            rows.constraint_inertia.store(world, rows_packed, constants.dof_inverse_weight.load(joint_dof));
        }
        rows_packed += 1;
    }
    // The floor's rows, when the worlds have a floor and the model's constraints are simulated - robot.zig makes no row
    // of any kind for a model whose options disable its constraints, its floor's contacts included.
    const floor_makes_rows: bool = params.options.floor_contacts == .enabled and params.options.constraints == .enabled;
    if (floor_makes_rows) {
        rows_packed = packContactRows(world, rows_packed);
    }
    integers.constraint_row_count.store(world, 0, rows_packed);
}

A row's Jacobian

Each row also carries its JACOBIAN J: how fast the row's distance changes with each dof's velocity. A limit's distance depends on one coordinate only, so its row has a single nonzero, at its joint's dof - +1 for a lower end, where the distance grows as the joint moves up, and -1 for an upper end:

   dofs of the KUKA               0    1    2    3    4    5    6
   the lower end of joint 2       0    0   +1    0    0    0    0
   the upper end of joint 5       0    0    0    0    0   -1    0

Rows are mostly zeros - a contact's row is nonzero only along the two bodies' chains - so they are kept SPARSE, in the form GPUs favour, ELLPACK: every row has the same number of SLOTS, each a dof and its value, filled from the first in dof order. Every slot's place is fixed by the model, as every row's is, so no invocation has to reserve room for another. A limit's row uses one slot.

Each row's projection

The solver needs five things of every row, computed from the row, the mechanism's inertia and its velocity, one invocation per world and row - robot.zig's projectConstraints, in its arithmetic:

   inertia         how hard the row is to accelerate: from the reference pose, or exactly J M^-1 J^T
   M^-1 J^T        for the exact inertia, the joint accelerations a unit push along the row would cause: J solved
                   with the mass matrix's factor (§49)
   velocity        J qdot - how fast the row's distance is changing
   regulariser     R = (1 - impedance) / impedance x inertia - the row's softness, as the solver uses it
   target          -b x velocity - k x impedance x violation - the acceleration a damped spring would give it

WHICH INERTIA. MuJoCo's default measures every row against the inertia the mechanism has at its REFERENCE POSE: each body's and each joint's inverse weight, computed once when the model is built (MuJoCo's body_invweight0 and dof_invweight0). A limit takes its joint's weight; a contact's edge takes the translation weights of its two bodies, times 1 + mu2. Nothing is solved, per row or per step. MuJoCo's DIAGEXACT option instead solves J M-1 JT for every row, every step, so the softness is set against the inertia the mechanism has now - an arm stretched out is harder to stop than one folded in. robot.zig and the device offer both (Options.constraint_inertia), and Geno's physics takes MuJoCo's default: for his rows the reference pose's inertia stays close to the exact one, and the solve per row is gone.

A row's softness is a time constant and a damping ratio (§30). The spring's stiffness k and damping b come from those two, the time constant never shorter than two steps, so the spring is one the step can follow.

An example: a lower end, the joint inside its margin by 0.025 and moving up, away from the end, at 0.1. The row's inertia is 0.5, its impedance curve at its maximum, 0.95, its time constant 0.02 s and its damping ratio 1:

   k = 1 / (0.95^2 x 0.02^2 x 1^2)  =  2770          b = 2 / (0.95 x 0.02)  =  105.3

   damping term      -105.3 x 0.1                =  -10.5      against the row's motion
   spring term       -2770 x 0.95 x (-0.025)     =  +65.8      out of the margin
   target                                        =   55.3

   regulariser       (1 - 0.95) / 0.95 x 0.5     =   0.026

The spring pushes the joint out of its margin harder than the damper holds back its motion. The solver will ask the row for its target acceleration, and the regulariser decides how far it may fall short.

project_constraints
/// EACH ROW'S PROJECTION, one invocation per world and row slot - robot.zig's `projectConstraints`, row by row, in its
/// arithmetic: everything the solver needs to know of a row, from the row, the mechanism's inertia and its velocity.
///
///     inertia             how hard the row is to accelerate: under MuJoCo's default, the reference pose's, which
///                         the rows kernel has set - no solve; under the exact inertia (MuJoCo's DIAGEXACT),
///                         J M^-1 J^T at the pose the mechanism has now, J's row solved with the world's mass-matrix
///                         factor into M^-1 J^T, the joint accelerations a unit push along the row would cause
///     velocity            J qdot - how fast the row's distance is changing
///     impedance           the row's impedance curve at its violation (`robot.Impedance.at`): how strongly the row
///                         holds, between the curve's minimum and maximum, kept just inside (0, 1)
///     regulariser         R = (1 - impedance) / impedance x inertia - the row's softness as the solver uses it
///     target              the acceleration a damped spring would give the row: -b x velocity - k x impedance x
///                         violation, its stiffness k and damping b from the softness's time constant (never shorter
///                         than two steps) and damping ratio
///
/// Every row is a joint limit's, so its softness and impedance are its joint's. A row's Jacobian is sparse, and its
/// sums run over its nonzeros alone, in dof order: robot.zig's dense sums add only products of zero besides, which
/// change no sum, so the two agree to the bit.
pub fn project_constraints(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.constraint_capacity);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const row: u32 = handled.element;
    const integers: WorldIntegersLayout = params.layout.world_u;
    const row_count: u32 = integers.constraint_row_count.load(world, 0);
    // A slot past the world's rows this step: nothing to project.
    if (row >= row_count) {
        return;
    }
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const rows: ConstraintRowsLayout = params.layout.efc_f;
    const dof_count: u32 = params.counts.dof_count;
    const first_slot: u32 = row * params.counts.row_nonzero_capacity;
    const nonzero_count: u32 = integers.constraint_jacobian_nonzero_count.load(world, row);
    const uses_reference_inertia: bool = params.options.constraint_inertia == .reference_pose;
    var row_inertia: f32 = 0.0;
    var slot: u32 = 0;
    if (uses_reference_inertia) {
        // MuJoCo's default: the row's inertia from the reference pose, set by the rows kernel - nothing to solve.
        row_inertia = rows.constraint_inertia.load(world, row);
    } else {
        // M^-1 J^T: the row's Jacobian spread out over every dof - zeros, then its nonzeros - and solved in place.
        const solution_first: u32 = row * dof_count;
        var dof: u32 = 0;
        while (dof < dof_count) : (dof += 1) {
            rows.constraint_inverse_inertia_jacobian.store(world, solution_first + dof, 0.0);
        }
        slot = 0;
        while (slot < nonzero_count) : (slot += 1) {
            const nonzero_dof: u32 = integers.constraint_jacobian_dofs.load(world, first_slot + slot);
            const nonzero_value: f32 = rows.constraint_jacobian_values.load(world, first_slot + slot);
            rows.constraint_inverse_inertia_jacobian.store(world, solution_first + nonzero_dof, nonzero_value);
        }
        const solve_storage: ConstraintRowSolve = .{ .world = world, .solution_first = solution_first };
        robot.solveLdl(solve_storage, dof_count);
        // The row's inertia J M^-1 J^T, over the row's nonzeros in dof order.
        slot = 0;
        while (slot < nonzero_count) : (slot += 1) {
            const nonzero_dof: u32 = integers.constraint_jacobian_dofs.load(world, first_slot + slot);
            const nonzero_value: f32 = rows.constraint_jacobian_values.load(world, first_slot + slot);
            const solution_item: u32 = solution_first + nonzero_dof;
            const inverse_inertia_entry: f32 = rows.constraint_inverse_inertia_jacobian.load(world, solution_item);
            row_inertia += nonzero_value * inverse_inertia_entry;
        }
    }
    // The row's velocity J qdot, over its nonzeros in dof order.
    var row_velocity: f32 = 0.0;
    slot = 0;
    while (slot < nonzero_count) : (slot += 1) {
        const nonzero_dof: u32 = integers.constraint_jacobian_dofs.load(world, first_slot + slot);
        const nonzero_value: f32 = rows.constraint_jacobian_values.load(world, first_slot + slot);
        const joint_velocity: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, nonzero_dof);
        row_velocity += nonzero_value * joint_velocity;
    }
    // The row's softness and impedance: a limit's, its joint's; a floor contact's, robot.zig's contact defaults (as
    // `pushFloorContacts` leaves them).
    const kind: robot.ConstraintKind = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(integers.constraint_row_kind.load(world, row)));
    var softness: robot.Softness = .{};
    var impedance_curve: robot.Impedance = .{};
    if (kind == .limit) {
        const candidate: u32 = integers.constraint_row_source.load(world, row);
        const joint: u32 = tables.limited_joints.load(candidate / 2);
        const softness_pair: [2]f32 = constants.joint_limit_softness.loadPair(joint);
        softness = .{ .time_const_s = softness_pair[0], .damp_ratio = softness_pair[1] };
        const impedance_first_number: u32 = constants.joint_limit_impedance.at(joint);
        impedance_curve = .{
            .min = loadNumber(.model_f, impedance_first_number),
            .max = loadNumber(.model_f, impedance_first_number + 1),
            .width = loadNumber(.model_f, impedance_first_number + 2),
            .midpoint = loadNumber(.model_f, impedance_first_number + 3),
            .power = loadNumber(.model_f, impedance_first_number + 4),
        };
    }
    const violation: f32 = rows.constraint_violation.load(world, row);
    // robot.zig's expressions, in its order.
    const impedance: f32 = clamp(impedance_curve.at(violation), robot.min_impedance, robot.max_impedance);
    const softness_ratio: f32 = (1.0 - impedance) / impedance;
    const regularizer: f32 = @max(robot.min_regularizer, softness_ratio * row_inertia);
    const largest_impedance: f32 = @max(impedance_curve.max, robot.min_impedance);
    const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
    const time_constant: f32 = @max(softness.time_const_s, 2.0 * timestep);
    const stiffness_denominator: f32 = largest_impedance * largest_impedance * time_constant * time_constant *
        softness.damp_ratio * softness.damp_ratio;
    const stiffness: f32 = 1.0 / @max(robot.min_impedance, stiffness_denominator);
    const damping: f32 = 2.0 / @max(robot.min_impedance, largest_impedance * time_constant);
    const damping_term: f32 = -damping * row_velocity;
    const stiffness_term: f32 = -stiffness * impedance * violation;
    const target_acceleration: f32 = damping_term + stiffness_term;
    if (!uses_reference_inertia) {
        rows.constraint_inertia.store(world, row, row_inertia);
    }
    rows.constraint_velocity.store(world, row, row_velocity);
    rows.constraint_regularizer.store(world, row, regularizer);
    rows.constraint_target_acceleration.store(world, row, target_acceleration);
}

The three kernels form the CONSTRAINT PROGRAM, listed beside the step's dispatches in robotgpu_program.zig.

57The constraint solver on many worlds

The rows of chapter 56 say what each constraint wants; the SOLVER decides what happens. It finds the joint accelerations that best reconcile the mechanism's inertia with every row's push - and from them, the force each row exerts. This chapter builds the solver up from one joint at its limit, follows Newton's method round by round on numbers small enough to check by hand, and then shows how a whole workgroup runs it for one world. Chapter 21 introduces the same method on the CPU, and compares it with Gauss-Seidel.

What the solver finds

Without constraints a world would accelerate at its FREE acceleration afree - what the forces of the earlier chapters produce through the mass matrix. Each active row asks for something else: that its own acceleration J a reach its target aref. The solver weighs the two with one cost:

   L(a)  =  1/2 (a - a_free)^T M (a - a_free)    +    sum over the active rows of  1/2 r^2 / R

            moving away from the free                 each row's miss, r = J a - aref, priced by its
            acceleration, priced by the inertia       softness R: the softer the row, the cheaper the miss

A row that may only push - a joint limit - is ACTIVE only while it would push, while r < 0: once the joint accelerates away from its end faster than the row asks, the row has nothing to do and leaves the cost. That makes L convex, and quadratic piece by piece: one quadratic for each set of active rows, joined where rows switch on or off. The solver's answer is L's lowest point.

One joint at its lower limit makes the shape plain. Its inertia is M = 1; gravity and its motors would accelerate it at afree = 2; its limit row (J = 1) asks for aref = 4, a firm push out of the margin, with a softness R = 1:

   L(a) = 1/2 (a - 2)^2  +  1/2 (a - 4)^2        while a < 4, the row active
   L(a) = 1/2 (a - 2)^2                          from a = 4 on, the row satisfied

          L
        5 |     .
        4 |                             .       L(5) = 4.5: past a = 4 the row has left,
        3 |        .                 .          and only the inertia's parabola rises
        2 |           .           .
        1 |              .  *  .
          +-----+-----+-----+-----+-----+--  a
                1     2     3     4     5

   lowest point  a = 3:   the row misses by r = -1, and the joint accelerates at 3, not 2

The answer sits between the two wishes: the free acceleration's 2 and the row's 4, nearer the free one by as much as the inertia outweighs the row's softness. A softer row (larger R) would give way more, a heavier joint (larger M) less.

Newton's method

On each of its pieces L is an exact quadratic, so the method that solves quadratics in one step is the natural one. NEWTON'S METHOD takes L's slope and curvature where it stands - the GRADIENT g and the HESSIAN H - and steps to where the quadratic with that slope and curvature is lowest:

   g  =  M (a - a_free)  +  J^T (r / R)            the active rows' sum, J^T stacking each row's r / R
   H  =  M  +  J^T R^-1 J                          each active row adds J_i^T J_i / R_i
   the step delta solves   H delta = -g

On the joint at its limit, from the free acceleration:

   a = 2      r = 2 - 4 = -2      the row is active
   g = 1 x (2 - 2) + 1 x (-2 / 1) = -2              downhill to the right
   H = 1 + 1 x 1 / 1              =  2
   delta = -g / H                 =  1              a = 3

   a = 3      r = -1      g = 1 x (3 - 2) + (-1 / 1) = 0      the lowest point, in one step

One step suffices because the step stayed on the piece it started from - the row active before and after. With many rows, a step can cross where some row switches on or off; there L is a different quadratic, and the full step can land somewhere worse. The method then searches along the step (below) and goes round again from where it lands, with the rows active there. Each round builds g and H afresh, and a handful of rounds reach the answer.

Solving for the step

H is symmetric and positive definite - M is, and each row adds a non-negative term - so it factors as H = L LT, L lower triangular: the CHOLESKY factorisation. The step then takes two triangular solves, one forward (L y = -g) and one backward (LT delta = y).

The factorisation goes column by column, and each column has three parts: the PIVOT, the square root of the column's diagonal entry; the COLUMN below it, divided by the pivot; and the TRAILING UPDATE, every entry still to come less the product of its row's and its column's new factor entries. On a small matrix with whole numbers:

        | 4  2  2 |
   H =  | 2  5  3 |
        | 2  3  6 |

   column 0   pivot      L00 = sqrt 4 = 2
              column     L10 = 2 / 2 = 1         L20 = 2 / 2 = 1
              update     h11 = 5 - 1 x 1 = 4     h21 = 3 - 1 x 1 = 2     h22 = 6 - 1 x 1 = 5

   column 1   pivot      L11 = sqrt 4 = 2
              column     L21 = 2 / 2 = 1
              update     h22 = 5 - 1 x 1 = 4

   column 2   pivot      L22 = sqrt 4 = 2

        | 2  .  . |
   L =  | 1  2  . |      L L^T = H
        | 1  1  2 |

Every entry receives its subtractions in column order, one per earlier column - exactly the running subtraction of robot.zig's row-by-row factorisation, so the two produce the same numbers to the bit, though they visit the entries in different orders. If a pivot's entry is not positive - a degenerate mechanism, a rounding accident - the factorisation has failed, and the round falls back to the plain downhill direction, delta = -g, still a step that lowers L, only less directly.

Where Newton starts

Newton begins from the free acceleration, or from the last step's answer - MuJoCo's WARM START - whichever costs less, the warm start kept when the two cost the same. Each has its strength. The last answer is close when little changes from step to step: a robot standing still solves the same problem again. The free acceleration has the right rows active when much changes: every row that will push is pushing there, since without the constraints the joints run straight into them, so the first step from it is nearly the answer. Comparing their costs takes whichever is better for this step.

The line search

Because a step can cross into another piece of the cost, it is not taken whole without looking. Along the line a + alpha delta the cost is a quadratic in alpha piece by piece, its pieces joined where a row turns on or off, so its lowest point can be found exactly. Everything it depends on is gathered once: the inertia's part comes from one product M delta, and each row's from its residual r and its rate J delta along the step. From those, the cost at any alpha - measured from its value at alpha = 0, so that a small fall is never lost in the rounding of two large costs - and its slope and curvature are one pass over the rows.

The search is Newton's method in alpha: from the start, a step to -slope / curvature. If the slope there is near zero and the cost has fallen, that is the answer. Otherwise the two points BRACKET the lowest point - one sloping down, one up - and each iteration tries three candidates, a Newton step from each end and the midpoint, moving an end to any candidate whose slope has the same sign and is nearer zero, until one has converged. An example, the joint of the last section with its round standing at a = 5: moving away from its limit, its row idle, so g = 3, H = 1 and delta = -3. Along the line the row turns on at alpha = 1/3, where a reaches 4:

   alpha < 1/3:   cost change  -9 alpha + 4.5 alpha^2                      the inertia's part alone
   alpha > 1/3:   cost change   9 alpha^2 - 12 alpha + 0.5                   the row pushing too

   at 0:          slope -9, curvature 9    a Newton step to alpha = 1
   at 1:          slope +6                 past the lowest point: the bracket is [0, 1]
   from 1:        1 - 6 / 18 = 2/3         slope 0 - the answer, a = 5 - 3 x 2/3 = 3

The first Newton step used the curvature where the row was idle and overshot past the turn; the step back from the far end used the curvature with the row pushing, and landed exactly. Two pieces, five points evaluated, one round.

When to stop

The rounds stop at the first of four signs, each measured against the mechanism's inertia - scaled by 1 / trace(M), so that one tolerance means the same for a light arm and a heavy humanoid: the line search found no step; the cost fell by too little to matter; the gradient is short enough; or the fall the quadratic model predicts for a full step - half the Newton decrement, gT H-1 g - is small. A start that already meets the gradient's rule takes no round at all. Otherwise they stop at the solver's limit of rounds: for Geno three, with at most five iterations of each line search - most steps end in one or two, and the limit cuts only the slow tail.

Forces from the answer

The answer's accelerations carry the forces with them. Each active row's force is its miss over its softness, f = -r / R, held at zero or above for a row that may only push; the joint forces the rows exert are JT f, and with them the equation of motion balances:

   M a  =  M a_free  +  J^T f

   the joint at its limit:   r = -1,  f = -(-1) / 1 = 1
                             M a = 1 x 3 = 3      M a_free + J f = 1 x 2 + 1 x 1 = 3

The row's unit of force is what lifted the joint's acceleration from 2 to 3: the limit pushing back.

The solve inside the step

The solve sits in the middle of every step. The smooth dynamics come first - kinematics, the mass matrix and its factor, the velocities, the forces - up to the free acceleration. Then the constraint program: the rows each world's limits and its floor's contacts make, their projection, and Newton's answer, which becomes the step's acceleration. A world with no row keeps its free acceleration, answered at once. Last, the integration: the velocity update, the joints' damping taken implicitly from M a - which holds the constraint forces as well as the smooth ones - and the advance of the positions. A model whose options disable its constraints makes no row, and steps as if it had none.

A workgroup per world

A world's solve is a few dozen numbers wide - Geno's Hessian is 75 by 75 - and a few rounds deep. On the device a whole WORKGROUP of 64 invocations solves one world, every round in one dispatch, its working set in workgroup memory: the Hessian; seven vectors of the round (the acceleration, the inertia's gradient M (a - a_free), the gradient, the step, a product with M, the joint forces and a scratch); and each row's residual, weight, rate along the step and force, whether it is active and what it stands on - 31,420 bytes for up to 76 dofs and 256 rows, the Hessian 23 KB of it. A WebGPU adapter commonly grants 32 KB and a desktop GPU 48, and the engine asks each adapter for all it has; a device that grants less is refused by name, and so is a model wider or with more possible rows than the working set holds.

WHAT A ROW STANDS ON, kept a word a row, makes its entry for any dof a single read. A limit's row has one nonzero, at its joint's dof. A contact's row has its body's chain of dofs, in dof order, so its entry for a dof sits at the dof's place in that chain - a table built with the model, each body's chain as each dof's place in it, or none. The Hessian's entries and the gradient's sums visit the rows in order and read each row's entry at once, so nothing searches a row, and every sum still takes its terms in robot.zig's order. Whether a row may only push follows from its kind, as in robot.zig. And of the Hessian only the lower triangle is built: the factorisation and the solves read no other.

Two of the working set's numbers are KEPT between rounds rather than computed again: each row's residual r = J a - aref and the inertia's gradient M (a - a_free), each moved by alpha times what the line search already needed - J delta and M delta - when the step is taken. A residual computed again would carry J a's rounding into every round; moved, it is exactly the one the line search modelled.

The invocations work in PHASES, each shared among them and closed by a barrier. Most phases split their work by row, by dof or by entry of the Hessian; a few belong to the first invocation alone - the sums whose order fixes their rounding, the two triangular solves, and the line search itself, which is a chain of decisions over sums taken in row order, and so is robot.zig's own search run over the workgroup's rows:

   phase                      shared out by    what it computes
   any row?                   first lane       a world without rows keeps its free acceleration
   start                      row, dof         each row's weight D = 1/R; a = a_free
   where to start             first lane       the warm start or the free acceleration, by their costs
   kept products              row, first lane  r = J a - aref; M (a - a_free); the scale 1 / trace(M)
   forces                     row              active or not; f = -D r
   gradient                   dof              J^T f, the rows in order; g = M (a - a_free) - J^T f
   Hessian                    entry            the lower triangle: M's entry, then each active row's term, in
                                               row order
   factor, per column         first lane,      the pivot; the column below it; the trailing update
                              row, entry
   step and measures          first lane       -g solved forward and backward; |g|; the Newton decrement
   go on?                     first lane       the stopping rules
   the line                   first lane, row  M delta and the line's coefficients; each row's J delta
   line search                first lane       the exact search, over the workgroup's rows
   advance                    dof, row         a, M (a - a_free) and r moved by alpha
   answer                     dof, row         the accelerations - the next step's warm start too - J^T f, f

Every decision - go on, stop, take this step - is made by the first invocation and read by all together through a uniform workgroup value (the GPU compute tutorial's section 5.5), so every loop with barriers inside stays in uniform control flow. The twin runs the same phases: the workgroup's first invocation runs each phase for every lane in turn, which is what a barrier means - and so the twin matches robot.zig's solver to the bit, and the device matches the twin within the rounding of chapter 58.

newton_solve
/// NEWTON, ONE WORKGROUP PER WORLD, every round in this one dispatch - robot.zig's `solveNewton` from a cold start,
/// phase by phase (the section's comment above).
pub fn newton_solve(invocation: u32) void {
    const workgroup_size: u32 = config.workgroup;
    const world: u32 = invocation / workgroup_size;
    const lane: u32 = invocation % workgroup_size;
    if (!k.is_gpu and lane != 0) {
        return;
    }
    var context: NewtonContext = .{ .world = world };
    runPhase(lane, newtonDecideRows, context);
    // A BRANCH, NOT AN EARLY RETURN: the decision is uniform - every lane reads it through `workgroupUniformLoad` -
    // but a barrier after a `return` is refused by the WGSL translator (`spv2wgsl`), which cannot see that.
    const has_rows: bool = k.workgroupUniformLoad(u32, 1, newton_decision, 0) != 0;
    if (has_rows) {
        runPhase(lane, newtonStartRows, context);
        runPhase(lane, newtonChooseStart, context);
        runPhase(lane, newtonStartResiduals, context);
