        runPhase(lane, newtonStartInertia, context);
        newtonDerivatives(lane, context);
        runPhase(lane, newtonDecideStart, context);
        var iteration: u32 = 0;
        while (iteration < params.options.newton_max_iterations) {
            if (k.workgroupUniformLoad(u32, 1, newton_decision, 0) == 0) {
                break;
            }
            runPhase(lane, newtonLineInertia, context);
            runPhase(lane, newtonLineRates, context);
            runPhase(lane, newtonLineSearch, context);
            runPhase(lane, newtonAdvance, context);
            iteration += 1;
            newtonDerivatives(lane, context);
            runPhase(lane, newtonDecideRound, context);
        }
        context.iterations = iteration;
        runPhase(lane, newtonStore, context);
    } else {
        runPhase(lane, newtonStoreFree, context);
    }
}
runPhase
/// ONE PHASE of a kernel whose workgroup's invocations work together, run for every lane. On the GPU each invocation
/// runs it for its own lane and waits at a barrier for the rest; on the CPU twin the workgroup's first invocation runs
/// it for every lane in turn (the kernel sends its other invocations away) - a barrier's meaning exactly, every lane
/// done before any starts the next phase. So a phase keeps nothing of a lane's from one phase to the next outside
/// workgroup arrays: on the twin, one invocation is every lane.
inline fn runPhase(
    lane: u32,
    comptime phase: fn (NewtonContext, u32) void,
    context: NewtonContext,
) void {
    if (k.is_gpu) {
        phase(context, lane);
        k.workgroupBarrier();
    } else {
        var each_lane: u32 = 0;
        while (each_lane < config.workgroup) : (each_lane += 1) {
            phase(context, each_lane);
        }
    }
}
newtonUpdateTrailing
/// The trailing update: every entry (i, j), column < j <= i, less L_i,column x L_j,column - the column's subtraction
/// from each entry still to be finished, in column order.
fn newtonUpdateTrailing(context: NewtonContext, lane: u32) void {
    const dof_count: u32 = params.counts.dof_count;
    const column: u32 = context.column;
    const remaining: u32 = dof_count - column - 1;
    const pair_count: u32 = remaining * remaining;
    var pair: u32 = lane;
    while (pair < pair_count) : (pair += config.workgroup) {
        const row_dof: u32 = column + 1 + pair / remaining;
        const column_dof: u32 = column + 1 + pair % remaining;
        if (column_dof > row_dof) {
            continue;
        }
        const row_factor: f32 = newton_hessian[row_dof * dof_count + column];
        const column_factor: f32 = newton_hessian[column_dof * dof_count + column];
        const update_at: u32 = row_dof * dof_count + column_dof;
        newton_hessian[update_at] -= row_factor * column_factor;
    }
}

MuJoCo Warp's solver

MuJoCo Warp solves the same problem by the same method, in the same detail: the exact line search, the stopping rules scaled by the inertia, the residuals and M a kept between rounds, the warm start. Its organisation differs. It runs each round as kernels over every world at once, every world advancing in step until all have converged, where a workgroup here solves one world and stops on its own rule. It takes the warm start whenever a world has rows, where robot.zig and the device take, as MuJoCo does, the cheaper of the warm start and the free acceleration. And it updates the Hessian in place when only a few rows change state, instead of building it afresh.

58Checking the device

Every stage of these chapters is checked twice over: the twin against the CPU engine, and the device against the twin. The two checks ask different questions, and expect different answers.

   robot.zig            the CPU engine, the game's
       |                  to the bit: each kernel calls robot.zig's own functions, in robot.zig's
       |                  order of arithmetic
   the twin             the kernels, run as loops on the CPU (§45)
       |                  within each array's bar: the same arithmetic, rounded by another machine
   the device           the kernels on a GPU, through Vulkan or WebGPU

The first answer is exact because nothing differs: the same functions, the same order, the same processor. The second cannot be, and the rest of this chapter is about why, and about how close is close enough.

Where a GPU's numbers differ

A GPU computes in the same 32-bit floats, but its arithmetic is allowed to round differently in the last bit or two:

   division, square root     correctly rounded on the CPU; a few units in the last place on a GPU
   a x b + c                 two roundings on the CPU; one on a GPU that FUSES the multiply and the add
   exp, sin, acos, atan2     each GPU's own implementations, accurate to a few units in the last place

None of these is an error. Each is a correct rounding of the same real computation, made at a different point. But they compound: a step is thousands of operations, and a factorisation divides by its pivots, so the device's numbers and the twin's part by more than one bit - by a predictable amount.

The gap in epsilons

The device is judged against the twin in EPSILONS, the spacing of 32-bit floats near one (2-23, about 1.2 x 10-7), and against the size of the quantity each number belongs to, not of the number alone. A vector, a motion or an inertia is judged as a whole: an error that is small beside an inertia's large diagonal is small, even where it falls on a component close to zero.

largestGapEpsilons
/// THE GAP BETWEEN THE DEVICE'S NUMBERS AND THE TWIN'S, in epsilons - each difference measured against the size of the
/// quantity it belongs to, not of the number alone. A vector, a motion or an inertia is judged as a whole: an error
/// that is small beside the inertia's large diagonal is small, even where it falls on a component close to zero. So
/// the numbers go in groups of `numbers_sharing_scale` (zero: the whole of `numbers`, one world's block), and each
/// group's scale is its largest magnitude on the twin, at least 1:
///
///     group            [ 52.1   0.0004   -0.0002   12.3 ]           scale 52.1
///     gap of a number  |device - twin| / (epsilon * scale)          the largest over every group returned
pub fn largestGapEpsilons(
    device_numbers: []const f32,
    twin_numbers: []const f32,
    numbers_sharing_scale: u32,
) f32 {
    // f32's epsilon, 2^-23: the gap between 1 and the next float.
    const epsilon: f32 = 0x1p-23;
    const group_length: usize = if (numbers_sharing_scale == 0) twin_numbers.len else numbers_sharing_scale;
    var largest_gap: f32 = 0;
    var group_start: usize = 0;
    while (group_start < twin_numbers.len) : (group_start += group_length) {
        const group_end: usize = @min(group_start + group_length, twin_numbers.len);
        var scale: f32 = 1.0;
        for (twin_numbers[group_start..group_end]) |twin_number| {
            scale = @max(scale, @abs(twin_number));
        }
        const device_group: []const f32 = device_numbers[group_start..group_end];
        const twin_group: []const f32 = twin_numbers[group_start..group_end];
        for (device_group, twin_group) |device_number, twin_number| {
            const gap: f32 = @abs(device_number - twin_number) / (epsilon * scale);
            largest_gap = @max(largest_gap, gap);
        }
    }
    return largest_gap;
}

Each array the step writes has its own bar, and two devices - a software Vulkan on the CPU and an RTX 5070 - show how much room each needs. The largest gaps over Geno's 64 worlds after one step:

                                        software   RTX 5070   bar
   poses, the frame, the mass matrix    8.3        16.0       256     a few roundings each
   the velocities and the forces        22.7       25.1       256     a few roundings deeper
   the factor, its pivots, the solve    239        230        1024    the matrix's conditioning on top
   the velocity update                  67.4       115        1024    a second factorisation
   the advance                          0.5        1.0        1024    dt x the rates' rounding

A bar of 256 epsilons is about 3 x 10-5 of a quantity's size: room for any device's rounding, while a wrong kernel - an index off by one, a sign flipped - misses by thousands or by everything. The factorisation's bar is four times wider because solving with a matrix multiplies an input's error by up to its condition number.

Probes

The step's gaps say that the whole is within its bars; the PROBES say where the rounding comes from. Each maths and spatial function the kernels share - a quaternion product, a rotation, a spatial cross product, a six-term dot product, the arc functions - runs on many inputs on the device and on the twin, and is held to a bar of its own. Their gaps on the RTX 5070 rank the sources of difference: a six-term dot product at 16 epsilons, where fused multiply-adds round at other points; atan2 at 28 and acos at 11, each GPU's own implementations; a quaternion product, a rotation, an inertia's product under 4.

Workgroups and barriers

Some kernels give a whole problem to one WORKGROUP: its invocations share a block of fast memory and work through it together, a column at a time, a BARRIER between columns making every write of one column visible before the next is read. A Cholesky factorisation is the example: one workgroup per matrix, the matrix copied into shared memory, a barrier after each column.

A barrier has one rule: every invocation of the workgroup must reach it. An invocation that returned before it - a workgroup with no matrix to factor, say - leaves the barrier in NON-UNIFORM control flow, which WGSL refuses and SPIR-V leaves undefined. It does not matter that every invocation of that workgroup would have returned together: nothing in the code lets a compiler see so. The pattern that keeps a barrier safe guards the memory, not the barrier - a flag for whether the workgroup has work, the buffer reads and writes behind it, and every barrier reached by all:

cholesky
/// CHOLESKY, ONE WORKGROUP PER MATRIX: the symmetric positive-definite matrix `values[m]` (row-major, `matrix_order`
/// rows) factored into `L L^T`, L written over the lower triangle of `sums[m]`, the upper triangle left as it was -
/// as robot.zig's `choleskyFactor` leaves it. The shape of robotgpu's solver: one world's Hessian in shared memory,
/// the workgroup's 64 invocations factoring it together, column by column:
///
///     for each column c:
///       1. the pivot      L[c][c] = sqrt(a[c][c])                    one invocation          barrier
///       2. the column     L[i][c] = a[i][c] / L[c][c],  i > c         rows spread over lanes  barrier
///       3. the update     a[i][j] -= L[i][c] * L[j][c], c < j <= i    rows spread over lanes  barrier
///
/// Each a[i][j] loses its products L[i][c] L[j][c] one column at a time, in ascending c - the very order
/// `choleskyFactor` subtracts them in, row by row - so the device and the twin do the same arithmetic, apart from a
/// multiply the GPU's compiler may fuse into the subtraction. The loop bounds are uniform (the order comes from
/// `Params`), no barrier sits inside a lane-dependent loop, and no invocation returns before one - a workgroup past the
/// matrices only skips its reads and writes - so every invocation reaches every barrier. The twin:
/// the workgroup's first invocation copies the matrix and factors it with robot.zig's own function.
pub fn cholesky(invocation: u32) void {
    const workgroup_size: u32 = config.workgroup;
    const lane: u32 = invocation % workgroup_size;
    const matrix_index: u32 = invocation / workgroup_size;
    // A workgroup past the matrices still reaches every barrier: only its reads and writes of the buffers are skipped.
    // A barrier must be reached by the whole workgroup, and one after an early return is in non-uniform control flow -
    // even when every invocation of the workgroup would return together, nothing lets the compiler see it, so WGSL
    // refuses it and SPIR-V leaves it undefined.
    const holds_a_matrix: bool = matrix_index < params.matrix_count;
    const order: u32 = params.matrix_order;
    const element_count: u32 = order * order;
    const matrix_start: u32 = matrix_index * element_count;
    if (k.is_gpu) {
        var element: u32 = lane;
        if (holds_a_matrix) {
            while (element < element_count) : (element += workgroup_size) {
                shared_matrix[element] = values[matrix_start + element];
            }
        }
        k.workgroupBarrier();
        var column: u32 = 0;
        while (column < order) : (column += 1) {
            const pivot_at: u32 = column * order + column;
            if (lane == 0) {
                shared_matrix[pivot_at] = @sqrt(shared_matrix[pivot_at]);
            }
            k.workgroupBarrier();
            const pivot: f32 = shared_matrix[pivot_at];
            var column_row: u32 = column + 1 + lane;
            while (column_row < order) : (column_row += workgroup_size) {
                const below_pivot_at: u32 = column_row * order + column;
                shared_matrix[below_pivot_at] = shared_matrix[below_pivot_at] / pivot;
            }
            k.workgroupBarrier();
            var update_row: u32 = column + 1 + lane;
            while (update_row < order) : (update_row += workgroup_size) {
                const row_factor: f32 = shared_matrix[update_row * order + column];
                var update_column: u32 = column + 1;
                while (update_column <= update_row) : (update_column += 1) {
                    const column_factor: f32 = shared_matrix[update_column * order + column];
                    const updated_at: u32 = update_row * order + update_column;
                    shared_matrix[updated_at] = shared_matrix[updated_at] - row_factor * column_factor;
                }
            }
            k.workgroupBarrier();
        }
        if (holds_a_matrix) {
            element = lane;
            while (element < element_count) : (element += workgroup_size) {
                sums[matrix_start + element] = shared_matrix[element];
            }
        }
    } else if (lane == 0 and holds_a_matrix) {
        const factored: []f32 = sums[matrix_start..][0..element_count];
        @memcpy(factored, values[matrix_start..][0..element_count]);
        _ = robot.choleskyFactor(factored, order);
    }
}

Many steps: the energy

One step is judged array by array. Many steps cannot be: each step's last-bit differences become the next step's inputs, and a body chaotic enough - a humanoid flailing under gravity - carries two trajectories apart however close they began. So over many steps the device is held to a physical quantity they should still agree on: the ENERGY.

The worlds are stepped under gravity alone - no applied force, no control, the dampers taken out - so that each world's energy can change only by the integrator's own error. Each world's energy is computed on the CPU, by robot.zig itself, from the device's state and from the twin's. The integrator's drift sets the scale: the device's energy must differ from the twin's by less than a hundredth of it.

worldEnergy
/// The energy of a world of `model` whose coordinates and velocities are `positions` and `velocities`: robot.zig's
/// `energy`, after its kinematics, centre-of-mass frame and composite inertias have been computed in `data`.
fn worldEnergy(
    model: *const z.robot.Model,
    data: *z.robot.Data,
    positions: []const f32,
    velocities: []const f32,
) f32 {
    @memcpy(data.joint_positions, positions);
    @memcpy(data.joint_velocities, velocities);
    z.robot.kinematics(model, data);
    z.robot.comPos(model, data);
    z.robot.crb(model, data);
    return z.robot.energy(model, data);
}
   Geno's 64 worlds, 100 steps under gravity alone, relative to each world's starting energy:

   the twin's own drift                 7.08 x 10^-2        the integrator's error: the scale
   the bar                              7.08 x 10^-4        a hundredth of it
   the device's energy, software        2.15 x 10^-6
   the device's energy, RTX 5070        2.40 x 10^-6        some three hundred times inside the bar

The trajectories themselves stay close too - every array within a few hundred epsilons after the hundred steps - but it is the energy that would still mean something after a thousand.

Whole decisions

A decision of the tracking task is more than its physics steps: an observation, a judgement, pushes, new episodes (Pushes and new episodes). Its kernels decide things - whether a reference is lost, whether an episode ends, where the next one starts - and a decision made differently on the device is a different world from then on. So the tracking task is checked a whole decision at a time, and two rules shape the check.

No trajectory is compared. A GPU's numbers are the twin's only within rounding. A world whose error sits exactly at a limit could lose its reference on one and keep it on the other, and from that decision on the two would have nothing in common. So before each compared decision the device is given the twin's worlds whole - every world's arrays, numbers and integers - and each then runs that one decision. Every decision starts from one state on both, and the worlds are placed far from any limit: on their reference, or a metre away from it.

before each decision     the device's worlds = the twin's worlds, every array
the decision             one whole decision on each, at the same clock
after it                 integers: equal    numbers: within their bars

Integers exactly, numbers within a bar. A clip, a frame, a counter, a flag or a start's bin cannot be "close": an integer that differs is a kernel translated wrong, or a draw made at another decision's number. The numbers are held to bars, in epsilons as everywhere in this chapter, one bar per kind of quantity:

kindarrayslavapipeRTX 5070bar
positionsposes, the servo's targets, the bodies' lowest points87256
velocitiesvelocities, the observation3503674,096
the solveNewton's accelerations, kept as the next step's start1,8491,89516,384
actionschosen, given, applied, previous00.54
the judgementtracking terms, rewards, the episode's sums37281,024
the effortenergy and squared torques over the decision1551774,096

The two columns are the largest gap of each kind on lavapipe, a GPU emulated on the CPU, and on a desktop GPU. They differ by little: what separates a device from the twin is the order and the fusing of the arithmetic, which every device has, more than anything particular to one.

The velocities' gap is larger than a single step's: a push's direction is a cosine and a sine of a drawn angle, a start's noise a logarithm and a cosine, and a GPU rounds those its own way. The draws themselves are integers until their last operation, which is why the same worlds are pushed, and start again at the same frame, on both sides.

The check's worlds live through everything a decision can decide. One character is knocked a metre off and lives on: a loss, and an aftermath. It is then held on its reference for two decisions: a return. Another is knocked off with no aftermath: its episode ends, and it starts again at the frame the curriculum draws, resting on the floor, with its noise, in trouble. Every world still in its episode is pushed at every decision.

tracking_float_arrays
/// THE TRACKING TASK's FLOAT ARRAYS A WHOLE DECISION LEAVES: what each world carries to its next decision - its pose
/// and velocities, its warm start, its actions as chosen, given, applied and previous, its episode's sums - and the
/// decision's results - the servo's last targets, each body's lowest point, the observation, the tracking terms, the
/// rewards and the effort. EACH BAR is several times the gaps measured (in brackets: the largest of its kind over
/// the check's three decisions, on lavapipe and then on an RTX 5070), as the step's bars are: every GPU rounds its
/// own way - fused multiply-adds, its own sine, cosine and logarithm - and the two measured so far differ by little.
const tracking_float_arrays = [_]TrackedFloatArray{
    // Poses, targets and heights [8.2, 7.4]: the positions' kind, as the step's.
    .{ .name = "joint_positions", .bar_epsilons = 256 },
    .{ .name = "servo_targets", .bar_epsilons = 256 },
    .{ .name = "body_lowest_points", .bar_epsilons = 256 },
    // Velocities after two contact steps, a push's direction and a start's noise - each drawn through a cosine or a
    // logarithm - and the observation that reads them [350, 367].
    .{ .name = "joint_velocities", .bar_epsilons = 4096 },
    .{ .name = "observations", .bar_epsilons = 4096 },
    // Newton's accelerations, kept as the next step's start: the solve's own conditioning [1,849, 1,895].
    .{ .name = "warmstart_accelerations", .bar_epsilons = 16384 },
    // The actions: numbers the kernels copy or blend from what was uploaded [0, 0.5].
    .{ .name = "chosen_actions", .bar_epsilons = 4 },
    .{ .name = "actions", .bar_epsilons = 4 },
    .{ .name = "applied_actions", .bar_epsilons = 4 },
    .{ .name = "previous_actions", .bar_epsilons = 4 },
    // The judgement's measures and the episode's sums [37, 28].
    .{ .name = "tracking_errors", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "super_mimic_tracking", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "termination_rule", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "rewards", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "super_mimic_rewards", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "episode_discounted_returns", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "episode_discount_powers", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    .{ .name = "episode_reward_sums", .bar_epsilons = 1024 },
    // The effort's sums over the decision's physics steps: torques times velocities [155, 177].
    .{ .name = "effort_energy", .bar_epsilons = 4096 },
    .{ .name = "effort_torque_squares", .bar_epsilons = 4096 },
};
runRobotgpuTracking
/// THE TRACKING TASK, DEVICE AGAINST TWIN, BY WHOLE DECISIONS: Geno at his training physics on his get-up clip, eight
/// worlds - a fleet of them for the clip's reference tables, its curriculum's mixture and its settings
/// (`Fleet.trackingSettings`), as a trainer would set a device. A GPU's numbers are the twin's only within rounding,
/// and a world whose error sits at a limit can end on one and go on on the other, so NO TRAJECTORY IS COMPARED: before
/// each decision the device is given the twin's worlds whole (both world arenas), each runs the one decision
/// (`Sim.stepDecision`), and every array of the tracking task is compared - numbers by their gap in epsilons,
/// integers exactly. THE EVENTS, on worlds far from any limit: one character knocked a metre off lives on (a loss, an
/// aftermath), is then held on its reference (a return); another knocked off has no aftermath - its episode ends and
/// it starts again at the frame the curriculum draws, resting on the floor, with its noise, in trouble; every world
/// still in its episode is pushed each decision. THEN THE DEVICE ALONE, TIMED (`size.timed_decisions`): more whole
/// decisions back to back, each followed by the readback of every world's flags that a trainer's host makes before
/// the next - what a decision costs on this device at this many worlds, the learner's work apart.
fn runRobotgpuTracking(
    gpa: Allocator,
    device: wgpu.DeviceHandle,
    queue: wgpu.QueueHandle,
    coverage: *DeviceCoverage,
    size: TrackingSize,
) !TrackingOutcome {
    const entries: [robotgpu.kernels.len]robotgpu_host.Pipe.KernelWgsl = spirvEntries(robotgpu, robotgpu_spirv);
    const training_physics: z.robot.Options = z.robot_geno.physicsOptions();
    var geno: z.robot_mjcf.Imported = try z.robot_mjcf.importSource(gpa, z.robot_geno_model, training_physics);
    defer geno.deinit();
    const model: *z.robot.Model = &geno.model;
    var clip: z.robot_dance.Clip = try .fromBytes(gpa, getup_zclip);
    defer clip.deinit();
    // The device's servo steps at the model's timestep, the fleet's at its clip's frame time: the two made one.
    clip.frame_time = model.opt.timestep;
    // The clip's first two seconds: every frame carries about a thousand numbers of tables (its coordinates, its
    // reference's features and bodies), and the test build's model arena holds a few hundred frames of them
    // (`robotgpu_sizes_test.zig`). The clip's storage is unchanged; only its length is.
    const compared_frames: usize = 120;
    clip.frame_count = @min(clip.frame_count, compared_frames);
    const world_count: u32 = size.world_count;
    try expect(world_count >= 8);
    const physics_steps: u32 = 2;
    const fleet: *z.robot_track.Fleet = try z.robot_track.Fleet.init(gpa, model, &.{&clip}, .{
        .envs = world_count,
        .capacity = 32,
        .decide_every = physics_steps,
        .action_filter = 0.5,
        .stiffness_filter = 0.25,
        .limb_stiffness = true,
        // The first half of the characters live on through a loss; the others' episodes end at it.
        .aftermath_steps = 30,
        .return_steps = 2,
        .aftermath_share = 0.5,
        // Every character still in its episode pushed each decision, and every start in trouble.
        .pushes = .{ .every = 1, .rate_per_second = 4.0, .speed_min = 0.3, .speed_max = 0.6, .start_share = 1.0 },
        .start_noise = .{ .pose = 0.05, .velocity = 0.2 },
        .curriculum = .{ .bin_seconds = 0.5 },
    });
    defer fleet.deinit();
    fleet.options.task.rest_on_floor = true;
    // The clip as the device takes it: each frame the model's coordinates, the reference's features and bodies.
    const coordinate_count: usize = model.coordinate_count;
    const root_len: usize = clip.nq - coordinate_count;
    const poses: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, clip.frame_count * coordinate_count);
    defer gpa.free(poses);
    for (0..clip.frame_count) |frame| {
        @memcpy(poses[frame * coordinate_count ..][0..coordinate_count], clip.pose(frame)[root_len..]);
    }
    const features: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, clip.frame_count * z.robot_track.localSize(model.body_count));
    defer gpa.free(features);
    var scratch_data: z.robot.Data = try .init(gpa, model);
    defer scratch_data.deinit();
    var scratch_state: z.robot_track.State = try .init(gpa, model.body_count);
    defer scratch_state.deinit(gpa);
    fleet.clipReferenceFeatures(&clip, &scratch_data, &scratch_state, features);
    const reference_body_count: usize = clip.frame_count * model.body_count * z.robot.reference_body_numbers;
    const reference_bodies: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, reference_body_count);
    defer gpa.free(reference_bodies);
    fleet.clipReferenceBodies(&clip, &scratch_data, &scratch_state, reference_bodies);
    const bin_count: usize = fleet.bins_start[1] - fleet.bins_start[0];
    const clips = [_]robotgpu.ClipPoses{.{
        .poses = poses,
        .frame_count = @intCast(clip.frame_count),
        .frame_time = clip.frame_time,
        .curriculum_bins = @intCast(bin_count),
        .bin_frames = @intCast(z.robot_track.binFrames(&clip, fleet.options.curriculum)),
        .reference_features = features,
        .reference_bodies = reference_bodies,
    }};
    var on_device: robotgpu_host.Sim = try .initGpuTracking(gpa, device, queue, &entries, model, world_count, &clips);
    defer on_device.deinit();
    // This simulator's dispatches counted for the run's coverage, before it is freed.
    defer coverage.add(&on_device);
    var twin: robotgpu_host.Sim = try .initTwinTracking(gpa, model, world_count, &clips);
    defer twin.deinit();
    // The fleet's task on both, as a trainer mirrors it: one value, one call each.
    const settings: z.robot.TrackingSettings = fleet.trackingSettings(.{});
    try on_device.setTracking(gpa, settings);
    try twin.setTracking(gpa, settings);
    // The curriculum's round as a trainer's host makes it: the fleet's start-bin mixture, summed in order into each
    // simulator's running sums, which a new episode's start is drawn from.
    const mixture: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, bin_count);
    defer gpa.free(mixture);
    const softmax: []const f32 = fleet.bin_softmax[0..bin_count];
    const long_run: []const f32 = fleet.bin_history_weights[0..bin_count];
    z.robot.startBinMixture(fleet.options.curriculum.rule, softmax, long_run, mixture);
    var running_sum: f32 = 0;
    for (mixture, 0..) |probability, bin| {
        running_sum += probability;
        const sum_at: u32 = twin.layout.model_f.bin_running_sums.at(@intCast(bin));
        const sum = [1]f32{running_sum};
        on_device.pipe.uploadAt(.model_f, sum_at, &sum);
        twin.pipe.uploadAt(.model_f, sum_at, &sum);
    }
    // THE WORLDS' STARTS, given to the twin alone (the device takes the twin's worlds before each decision): each on
    // the clip at a frame of its own, at rest; two of them knocked a metre off - one that lives on through its loss,
    // one whose episode ends at it.
    // The second character is in the half that lives on; the third from the last is in the half that does not.
    const knocked_and_living_on: u32 = 1;
    const knocked_to_its_end: u32 = world_count - 3;
    // Start frames nine apart from the tenth, coming round again after nine worlds: every start leaves the compared
    // decisions' frames before the clip's end.
    const first_start_frame: u32 = 10;
    const start_frame_step: u32 = 9;
    const start_frame_span: u32 = 81;
    const last_frame_reached: u32 = first_start_frame + start_frame_span + 16;
    try expect(clip.frame_count > last_frame_reached);
    const still: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, model.dof_count);
    defer gpa.free(still);
    @memset(still, 0);
    const pose: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, coordinate_count);
    defer gpa.free(pose);
    var starting_world: u32 = 0;
    while (starting_world < world_count) : (starting_world += 1) {
        const frame: u32 = first_start_frame + (start_frame_step * starting_world) % start_frame_span;
        @memcpy(pose, poses[frame * coordinate_count ..][0..coordinate_count]);
        const is_knocked: bool = starting_world == knocked_and_living_on or starting_world == knocked_to_its_end;
        if (is_knocked) {
            pose[0] += 1.0;
        }
        twin.uploadWorldPositions(starting_world, pose);
        twin.uploadWorldArray("joint_velocities", starting_world, still);
        twin.setWorldClip(starting_world, 0, frame);
    }
    // Every world's state, both arenas whole, as read from each simulator.
    const float_count: u32 = twin.worldArenaEnd(.world_f);
    const integer_count: u32 = twin.worldArenaEnd(.world_u);
    const twin_floats: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, float_count);
    defer gpa.free(twin_floats);
    const device_floats: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, float_count);
    defer gpa.free(device_floats);
    const twin_integers: []u32 = try gpa.alloc(u32, integer_count);
    defer gpa.free(twin_integers);
    const device_integers: []u32 = try gpa.alloc(u32, integer_count);
    defer gpa.free(device_integers);
    const world_arrays: robotgpu.WorldStateLayout = twin.layout.world_f;
    const world_integers: robotgpu.WorldIntegersLayout = twin.layout.world_u;
    const velocities: robotgpu.WorldArray(.world_f, .dofs) = world_arrays.joint_velocities;
    const flags: robotgpu.WorldArray(.world_u, .single) = world_integers.episode_flags;
    const velocities_before_resets: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, velocities.stride * world_count);
    defer gpa.free(velocities_before_resets);
    const action_count: usize = twin.counts.action_count;
    const actions: []f32 = try gpa.alloc(f32, action_count);
    defer gpa.free(actions);
    var outcome: TrackingOutcome = .{
        .float_gaps_epsilons = @splat(0),
        .integer_differences = @splat(0),
        .episodes_restarted = 0,
        .aftermaths_begun = 0,
        .returns = 0,
        .worlds_pushed = 0,
        .timed_decisions = 0,
        .timed_seconds = 0,
        .timed_dispatches = 0,
        .reads_flags_every_decision = size.reads_flags_every_decision,
        .state_floats = float_count,
        .state_integers = integer_count,
        .constraint_floats = twin.worldArenaEnd(.efc_f),
        .world_count = world_count,
        .physics_steps = physics_steps,
    };
    const decision_count: u32 = if (size.compares_with_the_twin) 3 else 0;
    var decision: u32 = 0;
    while (decision < decision_count) : (decision += 1) {
        uploadTrackingActions(&twin, world_count, decision, actions);
        // From the second decision the character in its aftermath is held on its reference, at rest: after two such
        // decisions it has returned.
        const holds_now: bool = decision >= 1;
        if (holds_now) {
            const start_frame: u32 = first_start_frame + start_frame_step * knocked_and_living_on;
            const frame_now: u32 = start_frame + physics_steps * decision;
            const reference_pose: []const f32 = poses[frame_now * coordinate_count ..][0..coordinate_count];
            twin.uploadWorldPositions(knocked_and_living_on, reference_pose);
            twin.uploadWorldArray("joint_velocities", knocked_and_living_on, still);
        }
        // The device takes the twin's worlds whole, so the decision starts from one state on both.
        try giveDeviceTheTwinsWorlds(gpa, &twin, &on_device, twin_floats, twin_integers);
        const clock: robotgpu.DecisionClock = .{ .number = decision + 1, .push_schedule_share = 1.0 };
        on_device.stepDecision(physics_steps, clock);
        // The twin's decision in its parts, its velocities read before its resets: a world whose velocities the resets
        // changed without starting it again was pushed.
        twin.submitDecision(physics_steps);
        twin.submitDecisionEnd();
        try readWorldFloats(gpa, &twin, velocities.offset, velocities_before_resets);
        twin.runResets(clock);
        try readWorldFloats(gpa, &twin, 0, twin_floats);
        try readWorldIntegers(gpa, &twin, 0, twin_integers);
        try readWorldFloats(gpa, &on_device, 0, device_floats);
        try readWorldIntegers(gpa, &on_device, 0, device_integers);
        inline for (tracking_float_arrays, 0..) |tracked, index| {
            const array: @FieldType(robotgpu.WorldStateLayout, tracked.name) = @field(world_arrays, tracked.name);
            const array_length: usize = array.stride * world_count;
            const device_numbers: []const f32 = device_floats[array.offset..][0..array_length];
            const twin_numbers: []const f32 = twin_floats[array.offset..][0..array_length];
            const gap: f32 = robotgpu_host.largestGapEpsilons(device_numbers, twin_numbers, array.stride);
            outcome.float_gaps_epsilons[index] = @max(outcome.float_gaps_epsilons[index], gap);
        }
        inline for (tracking_integer_arrays, 0..) |array_name, index| {
            const array: @FieldType(robotgpu.WorldIntegersLayout, array_name) = @field(world_integers, array_name);
            const array_length: usize = array.stride * world_count;
            const device_numbers: []const u32 = device_integers[array.offset..][0..array_length];
            const twin_numbers: []const u32 = twin_integers[array.offset..][0..array_length];
            for (device_numbers, twin_numbers) |device_number, twin_number| {
                if (device_number != twin_number) {
                    outcome.integer_differences[index] += 1;
                }
            }
        }
        // What happened on the twin this decision.
        var judged_world: u32 = 0;
        while (judged_world < world_count) : (judged_world += 1) {
            const world_flags: u32 = twin_integers[flags.at(judged_world, 0)];
            const finished: bool = world_flags & robotgpu.episodeFlagBit(.finished) != 0;
            const aftermath_begins: bool = world_flags & robotgpu.episodeFlagBit(.aftermath_begins) != 0;
            const returned: bool = world_flags & robotgpu.episodeFlagBit(.returned) != 0;
            const first_before: usize = judged_world * velocities.stride;
            const before: []const f32 = velocities_before_resets[first_before..][0..velocities.stride];
            const after: []const f32 = twin_floats[velocities.at(judged_world, 0)..][0..velocities.stride];
            const resets_changed_velocities: bool = !eql(f32, before, after);
            if (finished) {
                outcome.episodes_restarted += 1;
            } else if (resets_changed_velocities) {
                outcome.worlds_pushed += 1;
            }
            if (aftermath_begins) {
                outcome.aftermaths_begun += 1;
            }
            if (returned) {
                outcome.returns += 1;
            }
        }
    }
    if (!size.compares_with_the_twin) {
        // No decision was compared, so the device has not been given its worlds yet: the twin's starts, with actions.
        uploadTrackingActions(&twin, world_count, 0, actions);
        try giveDeviceTheTwinsWorlds(gpa, &twin, &on_device, twin_floats, twin_integers);
    }
    // THE DEVICE ALONE, TIMED - FIRST ITS WORLD, AS A NIGHT's: a push every three seconds of a character's episode and
    // none at random, in place of the check's push at every decision, which fells most characters within a second.
    // The fleet's settings are built again from its changed options and given to the device.
    fleet.options.pushes.every = 90;
    fleet.options.pushes.rate_per_second = 0;
    try on_device.setTracking(gpa, fleet.trackingSettings(.{}));
    const flags_read: []u32 = try gpa.alloc(u32, flags.stride * world_count);
    defer gpa.free(flags_read);
    // THE WARM-UP: decisions that are not timed, then a read that waits for them. The clock then starts on worlds in
    // the middle of their episodes, at every size alike - a first decision from a pose at rest has no contact yet,
    // and costs a fraction of the ones that follow.
    var warmup_decision: u32 = 0;
    while (warmup_decision < size.warmup_decisions) : (warmup_decision += 1) {
        const warmup_clock: robotgpu.DecisionClock = .{
            .number = decision_count + 1 + warmup_decision,
            .push_schedule_share = 1.0,
        };
        on_device.stepDecision(physics_steps, warmup_clock);
    }
    try readWorldIntegers(gpa, &on_device, flags.offset, flags_read);
    // THE TIMED DECISIONS. The worlds keep their last actions and live through whatever comes - losses, ends, new
    // starts, pushes. After every decision the host reads every world's flags, as a trainer's round does before the
    // next - or, when the size says so, only once after the last decision, which shows what waiting for the GPU at
    // every decision costs. The last read waits for everything: the wall clock around the loop is the cost of the
    // decisions and nothing else.
    const first_timed_number: u32 = decision_count + 1 + size.warmup_decisions;
    const dispatches_before: u64 = dispatchesSoFar(&on_device);
    const timed_started_ms: f64 = z.native_os.monotonicMilliseconds();
    var timed_decision: u32 = 0;
    while (timed_decision < size.timed_decisions) : (timed_decision += 1) {
        const timed_clock: robotgpu.DecisionClock = .{
            .number = first_timed_number + timed_decision,
            .push_schedule_share = 1.0,
        };
        on_device.stepDecision(physics_steps, timed_clock);
        if (size.reads_flags_every_decision) {
            try readWorldIntegers(gpa, &on_device, flags.offset, flags_read);
        }
    }
    try readWorldIntegers(gpa, &on_device, flags.offset, flags_read);
    const timed_ms: f64 = z.native_os.monotonicMilliseconds() - timed_started_ms;
    outcome.timed_decisions = size.timed_decisions;
    outcome.timed_seconds = timed_ms / 1000.0;
    outcome.timed_dispatches = dispatchesSoFar(&on_device) - dispatches_before;
    if (size.profiles_kernels) {
        const profiled_clock: robotgpu.DecisionClock = .{
            .number = first_timed_number + size.timed_decisions,
            .push_schedule_share = 1.0,
        };
        try reportKernelTimes(gpa, &on_device, physics_steps, profiled_clock, flags_read);
    }
    return outcome;
}

Every kernel reaches a GPU. The CPU twin runs the kernels' own source, so a check on the twin says nothing about the code a device compiles from that source. The device test therefore counts every simulator's dispatches and fails if a kernel was never sent to a GPU - except the two that no model of the test has work for: one runs over a model's sites, the other over its actuators' activation states.

The lab page

The robotgpu_lab example shows these checks running: Geno's worlds on the device and on the twin, each check marked as agreeing or not. The step is shown with its largest gap and that array's bar. The constraint program is shown whole: whether its rows' integers are the twin's, Newton's rounds on each side, and every array's gap beside its bar, in red past it - so one look at the page on a new GPU says which quantity that GPU rounds apart, and by how much. Its numbers come back from the GPU a frame after they were asked for - a browser never waits for the device - and the page says how many frames each copy took. It runs on WebGPU in a browser and on Vulkan natively, from the same source.

59Contacts against the floor

A walking character touches one thing: the floor. Its contacts are made by MuJoCo's own functions for a plane against a sphere, a capsule, a box and a cylinder, written once in robot.zig - a robot on a flat floor gets its contacts from them (pushFloorContacts), each geom posed in the world from its body. Each function gives every contact the same three things: where it is, how far apart the two surfaces are - negative while they overlap - and its frame, the floor's normal and two tangents along which friction acts.

A sphere, and a capsule

A sphere touches when its centre's height over the plane is within its radius, plus a margin if the floor has one. The contact's distance is that height less the radius, and it sits at the midpoint between the sphere's lowest point and the plane. A capsule is a segment with a sphere's radius around it, so it is the sphere test at each end of the segment: two contacts when it lies flat, one when it is tilted, none in the air. A capsule's contacts turn their frames to its axis, so friction acts along the capsule and across it.

   a ball of radius 0.1, its centre 0.05 above the floor:
      height 0.05,  distance 0.05 - 0.1 = -0.05          0.05 into the floor
      the lowest point at -0.05, the floor at 0:          the contact at -0.025, halfway

   a capsule, half a segment 0.2 and radius 0.05, tilted 45 degrees, its centre 0.17 up:
      the low end at 0.17 - 0.2 x 0.707 = 0.029           distance 0.029 - 0.05 = -0.021
      the high end at 0.31                                no contact

A box

A box's eight corners are taken in a fixed order - the first bit of a corner's number chooses the sign of x, the second of y, the third of z - and each corner on the box's lower half that is within the margin of the floor is a contact, at most four, the first four in that order. A contact sits at its corner moved back toward the floor by half its distance. A box resting flat touches at its four lower corners, corners 0 to 3; tilted onto an edge, at two.

planeBox
/// The plane against a box (MuJoCo's `mjc_PlaneBox`): its eight corners in index order - bit 0 picks the sign of x,
/// bit 1 of y, bit 2 of z - each a contact while it lies on the box's lower half and within `margin` of the plane, at
/// most four, the first four in that order. A contact sits at its corner moved back along the normal by half its
/// distance.
pub fn planeBox(
    plane: Plane,
    center: Vec,
    rotation: Quat,
    half_extent: Vec,
    margin: f32,
) [4]?PlaneContact {
    const offset: Vec = center - plane.point;
    const center_height: f32 = dot3(offset, plane.normal);
    var contacts: [4]?PlaneContact = @splat(null);
    var count: usize = 0;
    for (0..8) |corner| {
        const x: f32 = if (corner & 1 != 0) half_extent[0] else -half_extent[0];
        const y: f32 = if (corner & 2 != 0) half_extent[1] else -half_extent[1];
        const z: f32 = if (corner & 4 != 0) half_extent[2] else -half_extent[2];
        const corner_offset: Vec = rotate(rotation, vec(x, y, z));
        const corner_height: f32 = dot3(plane.normal, corner_offset);
        // Too far from the plane, or on the box's upper half: no contact from this corner.
        if (center_height + corner_height > margin or corner_height > 0) {
            continue;
        }
        const distance: f32 = center_height + corner_height;
        const back: f32 = -distance / 2.0;
        contacts[count] = .{ .position = center + corner_offset + splat(back) * plane.normal, .distance = distance };
        count += 1;
        if (count == 4) {
            break;
        }
    }
    return contacts;
}

A cylinder

A cylinder touches a plane on the rims of its caps, and MuJoCo takes up to four points there. The first is the deepest: on the rim of the cap nearer the plane, on the side of the rim that faces the plane. The direction to it from the cap's center is the plane's downward normal with its part along the cylinder's axis removed, scaled to the radius. If that point is not within the margin, no point of the cylinder is, and there are no contacts. The second is the same side of the other cap's rim. The last two lie on the nearer cap's rim a third of the way round either side of the first, so the three make an equilateral triangle; a cylinder resting on its cap stands on those three points.

   the nearer cap, seen from below:                  side view, tilted:

                A                                       second
                 \                                      ___o___
                  \  120 degrees                      /       /
          center   +---------o first                /       /
                  /                                /       /
                 /                               _/_______/
                B                       floor  --o-------------
                                                first   (deepest)

The triangle's two points lie at one height: a third of the way round the rim, a point's height along the normal is the first point's multiplied by cos(120 degrees), which is -1/2, so the two are tested together. When the axis stands straight up the part to remove is all of the normal - the cap lies flat, every rim point as deep as every other - and the cylinder's own x axis is taken as the first point's direction, as MuJoCo takes its local x.

A worked example: a cylinder of radius 0.05 standing upright at (0.2, 0.1), its cap 2 mm into the floor. The cap lies flat, so the first point is taken along x, at (0.25, 0.1); A and B are a third of the way round either side, at x = 0.2 - 0.05 / 2 = 0.175 and y = 0.1 ± 0.05 · sin(120 degrees) = 0.1 ± 0.0433. All three are 2 mm in - distance -0.002 - and each contact sits halfway between the surfaces, 1 mm below the floor. The other cap is far above: three contacts, the second slot empty.

planeCylinder
/// The plane against a cylinder (MuJoCo's `mjc_PlaneCylinder`): up to four contacts on its rims, in MuJoCo's order,
/// the first deciding whether there are any.
///
///     FIRST    the deepest point - on the rim of the cap nearer the plane, on the side toward it: the cylinder's
///              center, plus half its axis toward the plane, plus its radius toward the plane
///     SECOND   the same side of the other cap's rim
///     A, B     two more points on the nearer cap's rim, a third of the way round either side of the first - with it
///              an equilateral triangle, so a cylinder resting on its cap stands on three points
///
/// Each is a contact only when it lies within `margin`, and none is when the first does not: no point of the cylinder
/// is nearer the plane than the first. A and B lie at one height, so they are tested together. The rim's direction
/// toward the plane is the plane's downward normal with its part along the axis removed, scaled to the radius; when the
/// axis is perpendicular to the plane that part is all of it - the cap lies flat, every rim point as deep - and the
/// cylinder's own x axis, `side`, is taken instead, as MuJoCo takes its local x. Each contact lies halfway between the
/// two surfaces, its frame left to `contactFrame` (MuJoCo leaves the tangent zero). `axis` is the cylinder's unit
/// direction in the world (zimr's cylinder lies along its local Y), `side` its local X in the world, `half_length` half
/// its length. The arithmetic follows MuJoCo's, operation by operation.
pub fn planeCylinder(
    plane: Plane,
    center: Vec,
    axis: Vec,
    side: Vec,
    half_length: f32,
    radius: f32,
    margin: f32,
) [4]?PlaneContact {
    var contacts: [4]?PlaneContact = .{ null, null, null, null };
    const normal: Vec = plane.normal;
    // The axis turned to point toward the plane: its part along the normal made negative.
    const axis_along_normal_as_given: f32 = dot3(normal, axis);
    const turn_axis: bool = axis_along_normal_as_given > 0;
    const unit_axis: Vec = if (turn_axis) -axis else axis;
    const axis_along_normal: f32 = if (turn_axis) -axis_along_normal_as_given else axis_along_normal_as_given;
    const center_height: f32 = dot3(center - plane.point, normal);
    // The rim's direction toward the plane: the downward normal less its part along the axis, scaled to the radius -
    // or, with the cap lying flat, the cylinder's own x axis. MuJoCo's smallest length, mjMINVAL, decides which.
    const smallest_length: f32 = 1.0e-15;
    const rim_direction: Vec = splat(axis_along_normal) * unit_axis - normal;
    const rim_length_squared: f32 = dot3(rim_direction, rim_direction);
    const toward_rim: Vec = if (rim_length_squared >= smallest_length * smallest_length)
        rim_direction * splat(radius / @sqrt(rim_length_squared))
    else
        side * splat(radius);
    const rim_along_normal: f32 = dot3(toward_rim, normal);
    const half_axis: Vec = unit_axis * splat(half_length);
    const half_axis_along_normal: f32 = axis_along_normal * half_length;
    // FIRST: the deepest point. Above the margin, no point of the cylinder is within it.
    const first_distance: f32 = center_height + half_axis_along_normal + rim_along_normal;
    if (first_distance > margin) {
        return contacts;
    }
    const first_on_rim: Vec = center + toward_rim + half_axis;
    contacts[0] = .{ .position = first_on_rim + normal * splat(-first_distance * 0.5), .distance = first_distance };
    // SECOND: the same side of the other cap's rim.
    const second_distance: f32 = center_height - half_axis_along_normal + rim_along_normal;
    if (second_distance <= margin) {
        const second_on_rim: Vec = center + toward_rim - half_axis;
        const second_position: Vec = second_on_rim + normal * splat(-second_distance * 0.5);
        contacts[1] = .{ .position = second_position, .distance = second_distance };
    }
    // A AND B: the nearer cap's rim a third of the way round either side - their height along the normal half the
    // first's, the other way, as cos(120 degrees) is -1/2.
    const triangle_rim_along_normal: f32 = -rim_along_normal * 0.5;
    const triangle_distance: f32 = center_height + half_axis_along_normal + triangle_rim_along_normal;
    if (triangle_distance > margin) {
        return contacts;
    }
    // Sideways along the cap, perpendicular to both the axis and the first's direction, sin(120 degrees) of the radius
    // long: MuJoCo's normalisation, a multiplication by the inverse length.
    const sideways_direction: Vec = cross(toward_rim, half_axis);
    const sideways_length: f32 = length3(sideways_direction);
    const unit_sideways: Vec = if (sideways_length < smallest_length)
        vec(1, 0, 0)
    else
        sideways_direction * splat(1.0 / sideways_length);
    const sideways: Vec = unit_sideways * splat(radius * @sqrt(@as(f32, 3.0)) / 2.0);
    const back_along_rim: Vec = toward_rim * splat(-0.5);
    const below_triangle: Vec = normal * splat(-triangle_distance * 0.5);
    const point_a: Vec = center + sideways + half_axis + back_along_rim;
    const point_b: Vec = center - sideways + half_axis + back_along_rim;
    contacts[2] = .{ .position = point_a + below_triangle, .distance = triangle_distance };
    contacts[3] = .{ .position = point_b + below_triangle, .distance = triangle_distance };
    return contacts;
}

The frame, and the friction

A contact's frame is the floor's normal and two tangents. The first tangent is the capsule's axis when there is one, laid flat on the floor; otherwise the world's y axis, or its z when the normal is too close to y; either is made orthogonal to the normal, and the second tangent is the normal crossed with the first. On a flat floor with no hint the tangents are y and -x.

contactFrame
/// A contact's two tangents from its normal (MuJoCo's `mju_makeFrame`): the first the hint when it has one (a squared
/// length of at least 1/4), else the world's y - or its z when the normal is too close to y - made orthogonal to the
/// normal and of unit length; the second the normal crossed with the first.
pub fn contactFrame(normal: Vec, tangent_hint: Vec) [2]Vec {
    const x_axis: Vec = normalize3(normal);
    const hinted: bool = dot3(tangent_hint, tangent_hint) >= 0.25;
    const near_y: bool = x_axis[1] >= 0.5 or x_axis[1] <= -0.5;
    const fallback: Vec = if (near_y) vec(0, 0, 1) else vec(0, 1, 0);
    const y_start: Vec = if (hinted) tangent_hint else fallback;
    const y_axis: Vec = normalize3(y_start - splat(dot3(x_axis, y_start)) * x_axis);
    const z_axis: Vec = cross(x_axis, y_axis);
    return .{ y_axis, z_axis };
}

Two surfaces meet at a contact, and each has its own friction coefficient; the contact takes the larger of the two, as MuJoCo does for two geoms of the same priority. A foot of friction 1 on a floor of 2 grips at 2.

Every function writes its contacts into fixed places - a sphere one, a capsule two, a box four - and a robot's contacts come in the order of its geoms and, within each, of these places. The same pose always gives the same contacts in the same order, which is what lets a solver's work on them be repeated exactly, number for number.

On the device

robotgpu makes the same contacts with the same functions. Every world has the robot's slots - each moving geom's places, in geom order, laid out once when the model is packed - and one invocation per world and geom fills its geom's slots from the pose kinematics has just given it, or leaves them empty, every number zero. The floor is not part of a robot's model: whoever steps the worlds gives them one, its height, friction and margin (Sim.setFloor), and the device's floor is horizontal. With the same functions on the same poses, the device's contacts are robot.zig's - on the CPU twin to the bit, on a GPU within a few roundings - in the same slots and the same order:

floor_contacts
/// THE FLOOR'S CONTACTS, one invocation per world and geom - robot.zig's `pushFloorContacts` for one geom: MuJoCo's
/// plane functions, robot.zig's own (`robot.planeSphere`, `planeCapsule`, `planeBox`), on the geom's pose in the
/// world, each of its slots filled with a contact or left empty, in the order robot.zig pushes them. A capsule's axis
/// is its local y, zimr's convention. With no floor nothing is written: what reads the slots reads the switch first.
pub fn floor_contacts(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.geom_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    if (params.options.floor_contacts == .disabled) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const geom: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const slot_count: u32 = tables.geom_floor_slot_count.load(geom);
    if (slot_count == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const first_slot: u32 = tables.geom_first_floor_slot.load(geom);
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const up: Vec = vec(0, 0, 1);
    const plane: robot.Plane = .{ .point = vec(0, 0, params.options.floor_height), .normal = up };
    const margin: f32 = params.options.floor_margin;
    const center: Vec = state.geom_positions.loadVec(world, geom);
    const rotation: Vec = state.geom_orientations.loadVec(world, geom);
    const size: Vec = constants.geom_size.loadVec(geom);
    const geom_type: GeomType = typeOfGeom(geom);
    switch (geom_type) {
        .sphere => {
            const slot: ?robot.PlaneContact = robot.planeSphere(plane, center, size[0], margin);
            storeFloorSlot(world, first_slot, up, slot);
        },
        .capsule => {
            const axis: Vec = rotate(rotation, vec(0, 1, 0));
            const slots: [2]?robot.PlaneContact = robot.planeCapsule(plane, center, axis, size[1], size[0], margin);
            for (slots, 0..) |slot, index| {
                storeFloorSlot(world, first_slot + @as(u32, @intCast(index)), up, slot);
            }
        },
        .box => {
            const half_extent: Vec = vec(size[0], size[1], size[2]);
            const slots: [4]?robot.PlaneContact = robot.planeBox(plane, center, rotation, half_extent, margin);
            for (slots, 0..) |slot, index| {
                storeFloorSlot(world, first_slot + @as(u32, @intCast(index)), up, slot);
            }
        },
        .cylinder => {
            // zimr's cylinder lies along its local y; its local x is taken when its cap lies flat.
            const axis: Vec = rotate(rotation, vec(0, 1, 0));
            const side: Vec = rotate(rotation, vec(1, 0, 0));
            const slots: [4]?robot.PlaneContact =
                robot.planeCylinder(plane, center, axis, side, size[1], size[0], margin);
            for (slots, 0..) |slot, index| {
                storeFloorSlot(world, first_slot + @as(u32, @intCast(index)), up, slot);
            }
        },
        else => {},
    }
}

The step then makes each contact's rows, after the limits'. A contact at its margin or beyond makes none; any other makes four, one per edge of its friction pyramid (§20) - for each tangent and then each sign, the Jacobian along the normal plus the sign times the friction times the Jacobian along the tangent. The Jacobian is the contact point's on its body's chain of dofs: each dof's motion carried to the point, lin + ang × (point - origin), the origin the root's centre of mass, as robot.zig's jacPoint computes it. The friction is the larger of the shape's and the floor's, and under the reference pose's inertia (§56) a row's inertia is the body's translation weight times 1 + μ2. The rows come in robot.zig's order - by body, then by shape, then by slot, the order its solver sorts contacts in - so on the CPU twin the device's step on the floor is robot.zig's, to the bit:

packContactRows
/// THE FLOOR CONTACTS' ROWS of `world`, packed from row `first_row`: for each contact in slot order (robot.zig's
/// contact order, `packFloorSlots`), four rows - one per edge of its friction pyramid, for each tangent and then each
/// sign. Returns the rows packed so far.
fn packContactRows(world: u32, first_row: u32) u32 {
    var rows_packed: u32 = first_row;
    var slot: u32 = 0;
    while (slot < params.counts.floor_slot_count) : (slot += 1) {
        const contact: FloorContact = floorContactAt(world, slot) orelse continue;
        for (contact.tangents) |tangent| {
            for ([_]f32{ 1.0, -1.0 }) |sign| {
                storeContactEdgeRow(world, rows_packed, contact, tangent, sign);
                rows_packed += 1;
            }
        }
    }
    return rows_packed;
}

Each of those rows is one edge of the pyramid:

storeContactEdgeRow
/// ONE EDGE OF A CONTACT's FRICTION PYRAMID, as row `row` of `world` (robot.zig's `addContactRows`): pushing along the
/// edge is pushing along the normal while sliding along `tangent` in the direction of `sign`, so its Jacobian is the
/// normal's plus sign x mu x the tangent's. Each is the contact point's motion along that direction per unit of each
/// chain dof's velocity (robot.zig's `jacPoint`: lin + ang x the point from the origin), stored over the chain's dofs
/// in dof order. Under the reference pose's inertia, the row's is the contact's translation weight x (1 + mu^2).
fn storeContactEdgeRow(
    world: u32,
    row: u32,
    contact: FloorContact,
    tangent: Vec,
    sign: f32,
) void {
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const integers: WorldIntegersLayout = params.layout.world_u;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const rows: ConstraintRowsLayout = params.layout.efc_f;
    const normal: Vec = vec(0, 0, 1);
    const first_nonzero: u32 = row * params.counts.row_nonzero_capacity;
    integers.constraint_row_kind.store(world, row, @backingInt(robot.ConstraintKind.contact));
    integers.constraint_row_source.store(world, row, contact.slot);
    rows.constraint_violation.store(world, row, contact.violation);
    integers.constraint_jacobian_nonzero_count.store(world, row, contact.chain_length);
    var link: u32 = 0;
    while (link < contact.chain_length) : (link += 1) {
        const dof: u32 = tables.chain_dofs.load(contact.chain_start + link);
        const motion: robot.Motion = state.dof_axes.loadMotion(world, dof);
        const point_motion: Vec = motion.lin + cross(motion.ang, contact.point_from_origin);
        const along_normal: f32 = dot3(normal, point_motion);
        const along_tangent: f32 = dot3(tangent, point_motion);
        // robot.zig's expression, in its order: the normal's part, then sign x mu x the tangent's.
        const edge_value: f32 = along_normal + sign * contact.friction * along_tangent;
        integers.constraint_jacobian_dofs.store(world, first_nonzero + link, dof);
        rows.constraint_jacobian_values.store(world, first_nonzero + link, edge_value);
    }
    if (params.options.constraint_inertia == .reference_pose) {
        const cone_factor: f32 = 1.0 + contact.friction * contact.friction;
        rows.constraint_inertia.store(world, row, contact.translation_weight * cone_factor);
    }
}

60The servo on the device

The whole picture

Training a tracker on the GPU means running thousands of copies of the character at once, one per world, and teaching one policy from all of them. Every world follows the same path at each decision, all of them in parallel, and every number a world has lives in a few large arrays indexed by world - so one kernel, called once per world (or once per world and joint), does one stage for all of them:

   the policy's action          for every world, at once
          |
          v
   THE DECISION        the action filtered, turned into the servo's targets around the reference pose
          |            (each ball's target kept inside its rotation box), then the physics' steps -
          |            the servo's torques, contacts, constraints - and the effort it spent, summed
          v
   THE OBSERVATION     what the policy will see next: the character's state against its reference
          |
          v
   THE JUDGEMENT       how far each world is from its reference, the rewards it earned, and the
          |            episode's rule: tracking, lost, in its aftermath, returned, or ended
          v
   THE LEARNER         experience copied from the simulator's arrays into the learner's, on the GPU

Each stage, and where it is explained:

The decision (A decision) runs a decision's physics steps: the policy's action, smoothed by the filter, offsets the reference pose into the servo's targets; the servo turns targets into torques, and the physics of sections 55 to 59 advances the world. Each ball's target is first clamped into the rotation box drawn from the captures (The rotation boxes on the device).

The observation (The observation) measures what the policy reads next - the character's bodies in its own heading's frame, the reference's at the coming frames - with the same functions the CPU tracker uses.

The judgement (The judgement) compares each world with its reference: the tracking error, the tracker's reward and SuperMimic's (with its penalties for a jerky action, a joint past its limits and the effort spent), and the episode's rule, which decides whether the episode goes on.

The learner (One device for the physics and the learner) is a second pipeline on the same device. Experience reaches it by a copy on the GPU, so it never comes back to the CPU.

The twin. Every kernel is ordinary Zig, so the same kernels also run on the CPU, one invocation at a time, over the same arrays. That CPU twin is how each stage is checked: the twin's numbers against robot.zig's and the CPU fleet's, to the bit, and the device's against the twin's.

The ideas the stages carry out - the episode's rule, the curriculum that chooses where episodes start, the random draws any engine can make, the joint limits drawn from the captures - belong to tracking itself, and the robot-mocap tutorial explains them on the CPU fleet, where they are defined. This part shows how each runs on many worlds at once.

A tracking task drives its robot with a servo: each step, every joint is pulled toward a target pose - a frame of the motion being tracked - by a spring, and the model is asked for the torques that give the accelerations the springs want. The robot-mocap tutorial explains the law and why it is built so (its section 7, the spring solved implicitly in 7.2); here it is enough that it has three parts, each a robot.zig function, and that the device runs those same functions inside its step.

   servo_errors          per world and joint   how far each joint is from its target, a value per dof
                                               (one joint's differentiatePos)
   servo_accelerations   per world and dof     the acceleration a spring asks for at the END of the step -
                                               implicit, so a stiff spring stays stable - bounded
   servo_torques         per world             the torques that give those accelerations: M a + c, the mass
                                               matrix and the bias forces the step has just computed

The three run after the bias forces and before the smooth forces, which read the servo's torques as the world's applied forces. Nothing else of the step changes: a world with its servo off keeps the forces its host applied.

A FREE ROOT ASKS FOR ONE MORE SOLVE. Torques that assume the root holds still are a fixed base's; a free root accelerates, every joint torque pushing back on it, and the root has no actuator of its own. So its six rows of the equation of motion carry no torque, and they decide how the root itself accelerates:

   M_rr a_r + M_rj a_j + c_r = 0        a_r = -M_rr^-1 (c_r + M_rj a_j)

   M_rr   the 6 x 6 block of the mass matrix the root's six dofs share
   M_rj   how the joints' accelerations push on the root
   a_j    the joints' accelerations the springs want

The block is small enough to solve directly: Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting, then back substitution, in one function of values robot.zig and the device both call. With the root's acceleration found, the torques are M a + c over every dof, the root's six set to zero:

rootBlockSystem
/// A FREE ROOT's 6 x 6 BLOCK SYSTEM in world `world`, as robot.zig's `floatingBaseTorques` builds it: each row r the
/// mass matrix's root entries M[r][c], then its right-hand side -(c_r + M_rj a_j), the bias force less the joints'
/// wanted accelerations through their coupling to the root, taken over the joint dofs in order.
fn rootBlockSystem(world: u32) [6][7]f32 {
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const dof_count: u32 = params.counts.dof_count;
    var system: [6][7]f32 = undefined;
    var row: u32 = 0;
    while (row < 6) : (row += 1) {
        var right_hand_side: f32 = -state.bias_joint_forces.load(world, row);
        var joint_dof: u32 = 6;
        while (joint_dof < dof_count) : (joint_dof += 1) {
            // M[r][j] for a joint dof j below the root: its entry in j's row, at the root's column r.
            const coupling: f32 = massMatrixEntry(world, joint_dof, row);
            right_hand_side -= coupling * state.servo_accelerations.load(world, joint_dof);
        }
        var column: u32 = 0;
        while (column < 6) : (column += 1) {
            const higher: u32 = @max(row, column);
            const lower: u32 = @min(row, column);
            system[row][column] = massMatrixEntry(world, higher, lower);
        }
        system[row][6] = right_hand_side;
    }
    return system;
}
servo_torques
/// THE SERVO's TORQUES, one invocation per world - robot.zig's `servoTorques` after its accelerations. For a free root,
/// `floatingBaseTorques`: its 6 x 6 block from the mass matrix's root rows, its right-hand side -(c_r + M_rj a_j) taken
/// over the joints in dof order, solved for the root's own acceleration (`robot.rootBlockAcceleration`). Then
/// `inverseDynamics` on the whole: M a by robot.zig's product into the applied forces, the bias forces added, and a
/// free root's six torques zero - nothing actuates the root.
pub fn servo_torques(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    if (params.options.servo == .off) {
        return;
    }
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const dof_count: u32 = params.counts.dof_count;
    const root_dofs: u32 = servoRootDofCount();
    if (root_dofs == 6) {
        const root_acceleration: [6]f32 = robot.rootBlockAcceleration(rootBlockSystem(world));
        var root_dof: u32 = 0;
        while (root_dof < 6) : (root_dof += 1) {
            state.servo_accelerations.store(world, root_dof, root_acceleration[root_dof]);
        }
    }
    const product: ServoMassProduct = .{ .world = world };
    robot.multiplyByMassMatrix(product, dof_count);
    var dof: u32 = 0;
    while (dof < dof_count) : (dof += 1) {
        const torque: f32 = state.applied_joint_forces.load(world, dof) + state.bias_joint_forces.load(world, dof);
        state.applied_joint_forces.store(world, dof, torque);
    }
    dof = 0;
    while (dof < root_dofs) : (dof += 1) {
        state.applied_joint_forces.store(world, dof, 0.0);
    }
}

The servo's targets from the reference

A tracking task's servo aims at a clip. The clips live on the device beside the model: every frame of every clip a row of the model's coordinates, clip after clip, and for each clip its first row and its frame count - uploaded once, from the CPU's own clips. Each world holds which clip it follows, the frame it is at, and the action its policy gave it.

Before each physics step two kernels form every joint's target, exactly as the CPU tracker forms it (filterAction, applyAction, then clampHinges, all in robot.zig so the kernels call them). One invocation per world and number of the action filters first; then one per world and joint makes the target:

   0. the filter         the world's applied action moved toward its policy's by the task's share:
                         applied = f asked + (1 - f) applied - at 0.5 a new decision ramps in over
                         its physics steps (50%, 75%, 94% after one, two, four); 1 is none
   1. the reference      the clip's pose at the frame AFTER the world's - the servo aims where the
                         motion is going - held at the clip's last frame
   2. the action         the applied action, a displacement in velocity space, scaled, none on the
                         free root: integrated onto the reference over a step of one (a hinge's angle
                         added, a ball's quaternion turned by exp(a/2) on its right)
   3. the clamp          a hinge's or a slide's target held inside its range

After each physics step, one invocation per world moves its frame on by one - the clock that keeps the reference moving within a decision of several physics steps.

reference_targets
/// THE SERVO's TARGETS FROM THE REFERENCE, one invocation per world and joint - the joint's targets as robot_track's
/// `Fleet.driveOnce` forms them each physics step (`robot.applyAction`, then `robot.clampHinges`):
///
///   * the clip's pose at the frame AFTER the world's - the servo aims where the reference is going, as SuperTrack's
///     PD targets do - held at the clip's last frame;
///   * the world's applied action (`filter_actions`) added to it as a DISPLACEMENT IN VELOCITY SPACE: scaled by
///     `action_scale`, the free root's
///     six dofs none - a policy cannot push on the world - and integrated over a step of one
///     (`robot.integrateJointPosition`, which knows a hinge's offset is an angle added and a ball's is exp(a/2)
///     composed on its right, in the child's frame where its dofs live);
///   * a hinge's or a slide's target then held inside its range: a reference is in range, a reference plus an offset
///     need not be, and a target past a limit is a servo pushing against a wall.
pub fn reference_targets(invocation: u32) void {
    const handled: WorldElement = worldElementOf(invocation, params.counts.joint_count);
    if (!handled.in_range) {
        return;
    }
    const world: u32 = handled.world;
    const joint: u32 = handled.element;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const integers: WorldIntegersLayout = params.layout.world_u;
    // The frame the servo aims at: the one after the world's, held at the clip's last.
    const clip: u32 = integers.world_clip.load(world, 0);
    const frame: u32 = integers.world_frame.load(world, 0);
    const last_frame: u32 = tables.clip_frame_count.load(clip) - 1;
    const aimed_frame: u32 = @min(frame + 1, last_frame);
    const joint_type: robot.JointType = typeOfJoint(joint);
    const first_coordinate: u32 = tables.joint_first_coordinate.load(joint);
    const first_dof: u32 = tables.joint_first_dof.load(joint);
    // The aimed frame's row among every clip's frames: its clip's first row, and the frame within the clip.
    const row: u32 = tables.clip_first_frame.load(clip) + aimed_frame;
    const reference: robot.JointCoordinates = loadClipCoordinates(row, joint_type, first_coordinate);
    // The action as a displacement in velocity space: scaled, and none on the free root's dofs.
    const root_dofs: u32 = servoRootDofCount();
    const scale: f32 = params.options.action_scale;
    var displacement: robot.JointVelocity = .{};
    var dof_offset: u32 = 0;
    while (dof_offset < joint_type.dofCount()) : (dof_offset += 1) {
        const dof: u32 = first_dof + dof_offset;
        if (dof >= root_dofs) {
            displacement.numbers[dof_offset] = scale * state.applied_actions.load(world, dof - root_dofs);
        }
    }
    const displaced: robot.JointCoordinates = robot.integrateJointPosition(joint_type, reference, displacement, 1.0);
    // A hinge's or a slide's target inside its range, as `robot.clampHinges` holds it.
    const has_range: bool = tables.jnt_limited.load(joint) != 0;
    const held_in_range: bool = (joint_type == .hinge or joint_type == .slide) and has_range;
    // A ball's target inside its rotation box, as `robot.clampBalls` holds it (`robot.ballTargetInBox`).
    const boxes: ModelTable(.model_f, .joint_box_numbers) = constants.ball_boxes;
    const boxed: bool = joint_type == .ball and boxes.load(12 * joint + 3) == 1.0;
    const target: robot.JointCoordinates = if (held_in_range) blk: {
        const range: [2]f32 = constants.joint_range.loadPair(joint);
        break :blk .ofSingle(clamp(displaced.single(), range[0], range[1]));
    } else if (boxed) blk: {
        const box: robot.RotationBox = loadBallBox(boxes, joint);
        break :blk .ofBall(robot.ballTargetInBox(displaced.ballOrientation(), box));
    } else displaced;
    storeJointCoordinates(state.servo_targets, world, joint_type, first_coordinate, target);
}

A decision

A policy decides less often than the physics steps: a decision's action is held for several steps, while the reference moves on beneath it. On the device a whole decision is one batch of dispatches, so the host gives the actions and the GPU takes every step of the decision without waiting:

   the actions uploaded, once

   for each physics step of the decision:
       filter_actions        the applied action moved toward the policy's
       reference_targets     the servo's targets: the reference a frame ahead, the applied action on it
       the step program      the servo first, then the smooth dynamics, the constraints, the integration
       advance_frames        every world's reference a frame on

That is the order the CPU tracker takes a decision in, so a decision on the device and one on the CPU are the same computation, number for number.

submitDecision
/// ONE DECISION SUBMITTED as one batch: for each of its `physics_steps` steps, the tracking task's dispatches
/// before the step (the filter, the servo's targets), the step program, and those after it (each world's frame
/// advanced) - the order robot_track's `Fleet.step` takes a decision of `decide_every` physics steps. The actions
/// are the world's, uploaded before. Returns at once; the GPU works on.
pub fn submitDecision(sim: *Sim, physics_steps: u32) void {
    sim.pipe.beginBatch(sim.pipe.params);
    sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.decision_start_program);
    var physics_step: u32 = 0;
    while (physics_step < physics_steps) : (physics_step += 1) {
        sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.before_step_program);
        sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.step_program);
        sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.after_step_program);
    }
    sim.pipe.endBatch();
}

A stiffness per limb

The servo's spring has one frequency, 20 Hz, but it can be given a stiffness per limb: soft in some limbs, stiff in others, each limb's spring at its own frequency - the idea of variable-stiffness locomotion (Spoljaric, Yan and Lee), whose quadruped chose a stiffness per leg. A robot's limbs come from its tree: a body's limb starts at its first ancestor, or itself, whose parent branches. Geno's hips branch into his two legs and his spine, his chest into his two arms and his neck, so he has six limbs; a quadruped's trunk branches into its four legs; an arm on a fixed base never branches and is one limb.

limbStart
/// WHERE A BODY's LIMB STARTS (the limbs of variable-stiffness locomotion, after Spoljaric, Yan & Lee 2025): at its
/// first ancestor, or itself, whose parent branches - Geno's legs and spine start below his hips, his arms and neck
/// below his chest; the Go1's legs below its trunk. On a path that never branches below the world - an arm on a fixed
/// base, a chain on a free root - at the topmost body: the whole chain is one limb. Run when a model is set up, not in
/// a step: each ancestor's children are counted afresh.
pub fn limbStart(m: *const Model, body: u32) u32 {
    var current: u32 = body;
    var topmost: u32 = body;
    while (current != world_body) {
        const parent: u32 = m.body_parent[current];
        if (childCount(m, parent) > 1) {
            return current;
        }
        topmost = current;
        current = parent;
    }
    return topmost;
}

Each limb's stiffness is a number s in [-1, 1], and it sets that limb's spring frequency to f0 3^(s/4).
