A spring's stiffness goes as its frequency squared, so across s the limb's stiffness spans a factor of 3, centred in the logarithm on the tuned 20 Hz: from 15.2 Hz to 26.3 Hz. At s = 0 the servo is exactly the one without limbs. The damping ratio stays 1, critical, for every joint: the spring acts on accelerations and the mass matrix supplies each joint's own inertia.

limbSpringFrequency
/// A LIMB's SPRING FREQUENCY at stiffness `stiffness` (s, in [-1, 1] as a squashed policy gives it):
/// `frequency 3^(s/4)`.
/// A spring's stiffness goes as its frequency squared, so over s in [-1, 1] the limb's stiffness spans a factor of 3 -
/// Spoljaric, Yan & Lee's 20 to 60 - centred in the logarithm on `frequency`, the tuned law's; s = 0 is exactly it, as
/// 2^0 is 1. Through `@exp2`, the device's and the CPU's both.
pub fn limbSpringFrequency(frequency: f32, stiffness: f32) f32 {
    const quarter_log2_three: f32 = comptime @log2(@as(f32, 3.0)) / 4.0;
    return frequency * @exp2(stiffness * quarter_log2_three);
}

On the device the model carries each dof's limb as a table, and a world's action has room for one more number per limb after its joints' - that limb's stiffness, filtered with the rest before each physics step. With the task's limb stiffness on, the servo reads each dof's frequency from its limb's number; off, every spring is at the law's own.

dofSpringFrequency
/// Dof `dof`'s spring frequency in world `world` - `robot.LimbStiffness.dofFrequency` on the device: with the task's
/// limb stiffness on, its limb's at the stiffness the world's applied action gives it (the numbers after the joints',
/// one per limb); otherwise, and for the free root's dofs, the law's own.
fn dofSpringFrequency(world: u32, dof: u32) f32 {
    const options: StepOptions = params.options;
    if (options.limb_stiffness != .enabled) {
        return options.servo_frequency;
    }
    const limb: u32 = params.layout.model_u.dof_limb.load(dof);
    if (limb == robot.no_limb) {
        return options.servo_frequency;
    }
    const joint_numbers: u32 = params.counts.action_count - params.counts.limb_count;
    const stiffness: f32 = params.layout.world_f.applied_actions.load(world, joint_numbers + limb);
    return robot.limbSpringFrequency(options.servo_frequency, stiffness);
}

The observation

After a decision's last physics step, each world's policy is shown what it is to act on: the character, then the reference it is chasing, each in the root's frame. On the device that is one more short program. It first brings forward the quantities the observation reads to the state the step left - the bodies' poses, the subtrees' centres of mass, the dofs' axes and the bodies' spatial velocities, by the step's own kernels - then writes each world's observation, one invocation per world for each half: the character's, computed from the world's state, then the reference's, copied from a table.

   per body but the world, in the root's frame:
       position    3     the body's position less the root's
       rotation    6     the body's rotation, as the first two columns of its matrix
       velocity    3     the velocity of the body's frame origin
       angular     3     the body's angular velocity
       height      1     the body's height, in world
   then:
       up          3     the world's up, seen from the root

   laid out by kind: every body's position, then every body's rotation, and so on

Each body's numbers come from one function, localBody, which the CPU tracker's observation calls too, so the two halves are the same numbers on both. A body's velocity is that of its frame origin: robot.zig keeps every body's spatial velocity in one shared frame, with its origin at the root's subtree centre of mass, so a point of the body moves at the shared frame's linear velocity plus the angular velocity crossed with the point's offset from that origin.

observe_character
/// THE CHARACTER's HALF OF EACH WORLD's OBSERVATION, one invocation per world, once a decision - robot_track's
/// `local` of `stateOf`, the first half of what `observe` writes. Every body but the world, its features by
/// `robot.localBody` - its position less the root's, its rotation as two axes, its frame origin's velocity
/// (`robot.motionPointVelocity`) and its angular velocity, all turned into the root's frame, and its height - laid
/// out by kind as `local` lays them, then the world's up seen from the root. Reads the derived quantities the
/// observation program brings forward first: the bodies' poses, the subtrees' centres of mass, the bodies' spatial
/// velocities.
pub fn observe_character(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const tables: ModelTablesLayout = params.layout.model_u;
    const root: u32 = params.counts.root_body;
    const to_local: robot.Quat = conjugate(state.body_orientations.loadVec(world, root));
    const origin: Vec = state.body_positions.loadVec(world, root);
    // `local`'s layout: every body's position, then every body's rotation, velocity, angular velocity, height; the up.
    const character_bodies: u32 = params.counts.body_count - 1;
    const rotations_at: u32 = 3 * character_bodies;
    const velocities_at: u32 = 9 * character_bodies;
    const angular_at: u32 = 12 * character_bodies;
    const heights_at: u32 = 15 * character_bodies;
    const up_at: u32 = 16 * character_bodies;
    var body: u32 = 1;
    while (body < params.counts.body_count) : (body += 1) {
        const position: Vec = state.body_positions.loadVec(world, body);
        const motion: robot.Motion = state.body_velocities.loadMotion(world, body);
        const subtree_origin: Vec = state.subtree_centres_of_mass.loadVec(world, tables.body_root.load(body));
        const features: robot.LocalBody = robot.localBody(
            to_local,
            origin,
            position,
            state.body_orientations.loadVec(world, body),
            robot.motionPointVelocity(motion, subtree_origin, position),
            motion.ang,
        );
        const index: u32 = body - 1;
        storeObservationVector(world, 3 * index, features.position);
        var axis_number: u32 = 0;
        while (axis_number < 6) : (axis_number += 1) {
            state.observations.store(world, rotations_at + 6 * index + axis_number, features.rotation[axis_number]);
        }
        storeObservationVector(world, velocities_at + 3 * index, features.velocity);
        storeObservationVector(world, angular_at + 3 * index, features.angular);
        state.observations.store(world, heights_at + index, features.height);
    }
    // The world's up, in the root's frame: what tells the policy how the character is tilted.
    storeObservationVector(world, up_at, rotate(to_local, vec(0, 0, 1)));
}

The reference's half depends only on the clip and the frame, so it is computed once per clip, on the host, by the CPU tracker's own functions - the reference's state at each frame, in the root's frame - and kept as a table beside the clip's coordinates. Each decision, each world copies its frame's row after its character's half.

observe_reference
/// THE REFERENCE's HALF OF EACH WORLD's OBSERVATION, one invocation per world, once a decision - what robot_track's
/// `observe` writes second: the reference's state in the root's frame at the world's frame, its row computed once per
/// clip on the host (`ClipPoses.reference_features`) and copied after the character's half - the frame clamped to the
/// clip's last, as `referenceStateInto` clamps it.
pub fn observe_reference(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    // A simulator given no reference features keeps no table: its worlds' second half stays as it is, zero.
    if (params.counts.reference_feature_frames == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const constants: ModelConstantsLayout = params.layout.model_f;
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const row: u32 = referenceFrameRow(world);
    const row_size: u32 = @intCast(robot.localSize(params.counts.body_count));
    const first_feature: u32 = row * row_size;
    // The character's half comes first; the reference's follows it.
    const half_at: u32 = row_size;
    var number: u32 = 0;
    while (number < row_size) : (number += 1) {
        const feature: f32 = constants.clip_reference_features.load(first_feature + number);
        state.observations.store(world, half_at + number, feature);
    }
}

The judgement

After each decision, every world is judged as the CPU tracker judges its characters: how far the character is from its reference, what it earned for the decision, and whether its episode goes on. On the device the judgement is a program of its own, run after the observation, because it reads what the observation brought forward - the bodies' poses and velocities at the state the decision's last step left. The two always run together, observation first, through one call, so their order cannot be wrong; and that call refuses a simulator whose clips carry no reference tables, since without them nothing could ever end an episode.

submitDecisionEnd
/// A DECISION's END SUBMITTED, once a decision, after its last physics step - the only way to it, so the order
/// holds: each world's observation (`robotgpu.observe_program`), which brings the bodies' poses and velocities
/// forward, then its judgement (`robotgpu.judge_program`), which reads them. Asserts the clips' reference tables
/// exist: without them the judgement's kernels judge nothing, and no episode would ever end. Returns at once.
pub fn submitDecisionEnd(sim: *Sim) void {
    const tables_given: bool = sim.counts.reference_feature_frames > 0;
    assertf(tables_given, @src(), "submitDecisionEnd: the clips were given no reference tables", .{});
    sim.pipe.beginBatch(sim.pipe.params);
    sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.observe_program);
    sim.runDispatches(&robotgpu.judge_program);
    sim.pipe.endBatch();
}

Reading bodies on the device

robot.zig's tracking error is written once, generic over where the bodies are stored: it asks a storage for each body's position, rotation, velocity, angular velocity and lowest point, and the CPU hands it slices. The device hands it one storage type that knows which side it is reading. A world's side comes from the derived arrays: the pose, the velocity of the body's frame origin (the shared frame's linear velocity plus the angular velocity crossed with the offset from its origin), the angular velocity, and the body's lowest point, measured by robot.zig's own per-shape functions. The reference's side comes from a table row the host filled for that clip frame, twenty numbers a body - five vectors of four, so each reads as one. Both sides through one type, because the judgement takes one.

DeviceBodies
/// THE BODIES THE JUDGEMENT READS on the device (`robot.trackingErrorOver`'s storage; robot.zig's `BodySlices` on
/// the CPU) - one type for both of its sides, as the judgement takes one, and a field saying which:
///
///     a world's        each body's pose, the velocity of its frame origin (`robot.motionPointVelocity`, as
///                      `stateOf` takes it), its angular velocity and its lowest point - the derived quantities
///                      the decision's end brings forward
///     a reference's    a row of `clip_reference_bodies`, each body `robot.reference_body_numbers` numbers
///                      (position, rotation, velocity, angular velocity, lowest point)
const DeviceBodies = struct {
    source: Source,
    /// The world, for a world's bodies.
    world: u32 = 0,
    /// The row's first number in `clip_reference_bodies`, for a reference's.
    first_number: u32 = 0,

    const Source = enum { world, reference };

    /// One of the reference's five vectors for `body`: 0 position, 1 rotation, 2 velocity, 3 angular, 4 lowest.
    fn referenceVector(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize, vector: u32) Vec {
        const per_body: u32 = @intCast(robot.reference_body_numbers);
        const at: u32 = bodies.first_number + @as(u32, @intCast(body)) * per_body + 4 * vector;
        const table: ModelTable(.model_f, .clip_reference_body_numbers) = params.layout.model_f.clip_reference_bodies;
        // All four numbers: a rotation's w is the fourth (`vec` would set it to zero).
        const numbers: Vec = .{ table.load(at), table.load(at + 1), table.load(at + 2), table.load(at + 3) };
        return numbers;
    }

    pub fn count(bodies: DeviceBodies) usize {
        _ = bodies;
        return params.counts.body_count;
    }

    pub fn position(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize) Vec {
        if (bodies.source == .reference) {
            return bodies.referenceVector(body, 0);
        }
        return params.layout.world_f.body_positions.loadVec(bodies.world, @intCast(body));
    }

    pub fn rotation(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize) robot.Quat {
        if (bodies.source == .reference) {
            return bodies.referenceVector(body, 1);
        }
        return params.layout.world_f.body_orientations.loadVec(bodies.world, @intCast(body));
    }

    pub fn velocity(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize) Vec {
        if (bodies.source == .reference) {
            return bodies.referenceVector(body, 2);
        }
        const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
        const index: u32 = @intCast(body);
        const motion: robot.Motion = state.body_velocities.loadMotion(bodies.world, index);
        const root_of_body: u32 = params.layout.model_u.body_root.load(index);
        const origin: Vec = state.subtree_centres_of_mass.loadVec(bodies.world, root_of_body);
        return robot.motionPointVelocity(motion, origin, bodies.position(body));
    }

    pub fn angular(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize) Vec {
        if (bodies.source == .reference) {
            return bodies.referenceVector(body, 3);
        }
        return params.layout.world_f.body_velocities.loadMotion(bodies.world, @intCast(body)).ang;
    }

    pub fn lowest(bodies: DeviceBodies, body: usize) f32 {
        if (bodies.source == .reference) {
            return bodies.referenceVector(body, 4)[0];
        }
        return params.layout.world_f.body_lowest_points.load(bodies.world, @intCast(body));
    }
};

What a clip carries

A judged clip brings two tables beside its poses, both computed once per frame on the host by the CPU tracker's own functions: the reference's half of the observation, and the reference's bodies for the judgement. A world's row in either is its clip's first frame plus its own frame, held at the clip's last - one function computes it, for every reader. Either every clip carries the tables or none does.

Weights in a table, not the uniform

The judgement's weights - the tracker's reward weights, SuperMimic's scales, and the termination rule's limits - are set once and read every decision. A uniform would seem their place, but a uniform aligns every array element to sixteen bytes and a Zig extern struct cannot hold an array of vectors, so they live in a small model table instead: each struct's f32 fields in their declared order, written by one mapping that the CPU twin, the host's upload and the device's read all follow.

The episode's rule

Whether an episode goes on is one function, which the CPU fleet and the device both call. A character whose error is no longer finite is broken. Out of a short grace at its start, the termination rule is asked; its firing loses the reference. A character that keeps an aftermath lives on after the loss, and the rule is asked the other way round - is the reference held again? - counting a return streak; held long enough, the character has returned, the aftermath ends and the episode goes on. The episode ends at a loss with no aftermath, at a break, when an aftermath runs out, at the longest episode, or at the clip's end.

   decision   what happened                          counters after the judgement          verdict
      0       tracking its reference                 steps 1                               -
      1       knocked a metre off                    steps 2, aftermath left 30            lost, aftermath begins
      2       held on its reference                  aftermath left 29, streak 1           in aftermath
      3       held on its reference                  streak 2 = return steps: aftermath 0  returned - the episode goes on
      4       tracking its reference                 steps 5                               -
judgeEpisode
/// THE EPISODE JUDGED after a decision - grace, failure, aftermath, return and end, the one rule the CPU fleet and the
/// device both follow. A character whose error is no longer finite is BROKEN, grace or not (every comparison with NaN
/// is false, so the limits alone would let it run on). Out of grace and outside an aftermath, the termination rule
/// (`terminated`) is asked; its firing LOSES the reference - and a character that keeps an aftermath lives on through
/// one, the rule asked the other way round (is the reference held again?) until it RETURNS or the aftermath runs out.
/// The episode ends at a loss with no aftermath, at a break, when an aftermath runs out, at the longest episode, or
/// when `clip_over` (another decision would run past the clip). `counters` advanced in place.
pub fn judgeEpisode(
    counters: *EpisodeCounters,
    rules: EpisodeRules,
    err: TrackingError,
    limits: Termination,
    clip_over: bool,
) EpisodeVerdict {
    const finite_position: bool = err.pose_position == err.pose_position and err.pose_rotation == err.pose_rotation;
    const finite_root: bool = err.root_position == err.root_position and err.root_rotation == err.root_rotation;
    const broken: bool = !(finite_position and finite_root);
    const graced: bool = counters.steps < rules.grace_steps;
    const was_in_aftermath: bool = counters.aftermath_steps_left > 0;
    const rule_fires: bool = !graced and !was_in_aftermath and terminated(err, limits);
    const lost: bool = !was_in_aftermath and (broken or rule_fires);
    var returned: bool = false;
    if (was_in_aftermath) {
        counters.steps_since_loss += 1;
        const holds_again: bool = !broken and !terminated(err, limits);
        counters.return_streak = if (holds_again) counters.return_streak + 1 else 0;
        returned = rules.return_steps > 0 and counters.return_streak >= rules.return_steps;
    }
    const has_aftermath: bool = rules.aftermath_steps > 0 and rules.keeps_aftermath;
    const aftermath_begins: bool = rule_fires and !broken and has_aftermath;
    var return_length: u32 = 0;
    if (aftermath_begins) {
        counters.aftermath_steps_left = rules.aftermath_steps;
        counters.return_streak = 0;
        counters.steps_since_loss = 0;
    } else if (returned) {
        // Back with the reference: the aftermath ends here, and the episode goes on.
        counters.aftermath_steps_left = 0;
        return_length = counters.steps_since_loss;
    } else if (was_in_aftermath) {
        counters.aftermath_steps_left -= 1;
    }
    // An aftermath ends its episode only when it runs out - a failure to return.
    const aftermath_ran_out: bool = counters.aftermath_steps_left == 0;
    const aftermath_over: bool = was_in_aftermath and !returned and aftermath_ran_out;
    const capped: bool = counters.steps >= rules.max_steps;
    const ends_at_loss: bool = lost and !aftermath_begins;
    // A broken body ends its episode whenever it breaks, in an aftermath too.
    const finished: bool = ends_at_loss or broken or aftermath_over or capped or clip_over;
    const verdict: EpisodeVerdict = .{
        .in_aftermath = was_in_aftermath,
        .lost = lost,
        .returned = returned,
        .aftermath_begins = aftermath_begins,
        .finished = finished,
        .ends_at_loss = ends_at_loss,
        .capped = capped,
        .return_length = return_length,
    };
    return verdict;
}

The effort

Each physics step, right after the servo's torques and before integration, every actuated dof adds its effort to its world's sums: the energy, the torque times the velocity it acts through, braking counted as spending; and the torque's square against the character's weight. The reward reads them as means at the decision's end. They are measured from zero: a reference's own torques would need the reference's contact forces with the floor, which inverse dynamics with a free root does not have - a knee in stance holds the body's weight, which a contact-free calculation never sees.

addDofEffort
/// One dof's effort over one physics step, added into `sums` - the one arithmetic the CPU fleet and the device
/// follow, dof after dof in order. `weight` the character's mass times gravity.
pub fn addDofEffort(
    sums: *EffortSums,
    torque: f32,
    velocity: f32,
    timestep: f32,
    weight: f32,
) void {
    sums.energy += @abs(torque * velocity) * timestep;
    const relative: f32 = torque / weight;
    sums.torque_squares += relative * relative;
}
accumulate_effort
/// THE SERVO's EFFORT, one invocation per world, each physics step right after its torques - the CPU fleet's
/// `driveOnce` adding its own: every actuated dof's torque at the velocity it acts through (`robot.addDofEffort`, dof
/// after dof), added into the world's sums for its decision.
pub fn accumulate_effort(invocation: u32) void {
    const world: u32 = invocation;
    if (world >= params.counts.world_count) {
        return;
    }
    const state: WorldStateLayout = params.layout.world_f;
    const weight: f32 = params.layout.model_f.character_weight.load(0);
    const timestep: f32 = params.options.timestep;
    var sums: robot.EffortSums = .{
        .energy = state.effort_energy.load(world, 0),
        .torque_squares = state.effort_torque_squares.load(world, 0),
    };
    var dof: u32 = servoRootDofCount();
    while (dof < params.counts.dof_count) : (dof += 1) {
        const torque: f32 = state.applied_joint_forces.load(world, dof);
        const velocity: f32 = state.joint_velocities.load(world, dof);
        robot.addDofEffort(&sums, torque, velocity, timestep, weight);
    }
    state.effort_energy.store(world, 0, sums.energy);
    state.effort_torque_squares.store(world, 0, sums.torque_squares);
}

One call for the settings

Everything a CPU fleet's options and task say about tracking - the floor, the servo's law, the action's scale and filter, the limbs' stiffness, the head body, the episode's rules and the judgement's weights - reaches the device as one struct, through one call on each engine, so no setting is left at a default nobody chose.

SuperMimic's whole reward

Beside the tracker's own reward, the judgement computes SuperMimic's in full, as the CPU fleet sums it: its tracking terms, then three penalties. The action rate is each number's change since the previous decision, squared and summed; each world keeps its previous action for it, cleared at an episode's start. The joint-limit excess goes joint by joint: a ball by its rotation box, a ranged hinge or slide by its soft band. The effort's cost is the torque's mean over the actuated dofs and the decision's steps, and the energy per unit of the character's mass. Each penalty comes from the same robot.zig function the fleet calls, so the two sums agree to the bit. A joint's type is read through typeOfJoint, which loads the joint's packed word and decodes it in one place, so a joint's index can never be decoded as if it were its word.

effortCost
/// THE EFFORT's COST for a decision - SuperMimic's torque and energy terms, the CPU fleet's and the device's: the
/// torque's mean over the actuated dofs and the decision's physics steps, and the energy per unit of mass, each by its
/// weight.
pub fn effortCost(
    sums: EffortSums,
    scales: SuperMimicScales,
    actuated_dofs: f32,
    physics_steps: f32,
    mass: f32,
) f32 {
    const torque_mean: f32 = sums.torque_squares / (actuated_dofs * physics_steps);
    const energy_per_mass: f32 = sums.energy / mass;
    return scales.torque_weight * torque_mean + scales.energy_weight * energy_per_mass;
}

The rotation boxes on the device

The boxes the fleet draws from its captures reach the device as a model table, twelve numbers a joint - the box's low, its high and its center, four each, low's fourth marking a boxed ball - written in one call with the rest of the tracking settings. reference_targets clamps each boxed ball's target through robot.zig's ballTargetInBox, the very function the fleet clamps with, and the judgement charges the boxes' excess the same way.

loadBallBox
/// Joint `joint`'s rotation box from the table (`rotationBoxNumbers`' layout): its low, its high, its center.
fn loadBallBox(boxes: ModelTable(.model_f, .joint_box_numbers), joint: u32) robot.RotationBox {
    const at: u32 = 12 * joint;
    const low: Vec = vec(boxes.load(at), boxes.load(at + 1), boxes.load(at + 2));
    const high: Vec = vec(boxes.load(at + 4), boxes.load(at + 5), boxes.load(at + 6));
    const center: robot.Quat = .{ boxes.load(at + 8), boxes.load(at + 9), boxes.load(at + 10), boxes.load(at + 11) };
    return .{ .center = center, .low = low, .high = high };
}

Pushes and new episodes

A decision does not end with its judgement. Some worlds are pushed, and every world whose episode has ended begins another. Both happen on the device, so no world waits for the host between two decisions. One whole decision is five programs in a row:

the decision's physics steps     the actions held, the reference moving on beneath them
the observation                  every body's pose and velocity brought forward
the judgement                    tracking errors, rewards, whether each episode goes on or ends
the resets                       the pushes, then a new episode for every world whose episode ended
the observation, once more       a world that has just started is seen as it is now

The last line matters. The policy chooses its next action from the observation. Without observing again, a world that has just begun a new episode would be acted on from the last state of the episode that ended.

stepDecision
/// ONE WHOLE DECISION on the twin, as the fleet's `step` is one: the physics steps (`runDecision`), the judgement
/// (`runDecisionEnd`), then the restarts and the pushes (`runResets`, at `clock`). A world is compared with the
/// fleet's only between whole decisions - in between, one side may have restarted or pushed and the other not yet.
pub fn stepDecision(twin: CpuTwin, physics_steps: u32, clock: DecisionClock) void {
    twin.runDecision(physics_steps);
    twin.runDecisionEnd();
    twin.runResets(clock);
}

The reset program runs over every world, and each kernel acts only on the worlds it concerns:

kernelwhat it does
push_worldspushes worlds still in their episode: on a schedule of decisions, and at random at a rate per second
begin_resetfor a world whose episode ended, draws the new episode's clip and start frame
reset_poseputs the world in the clip's pose at that frame
rest_on_floorlifts the character until its lowest point rests just above the floor
perturb_startturns each joint by a small random angle and gives the root a small random velocity
rest_after_noiserests the turned pose on the floor again
start_in_troublefor a share of the starts, pushes the character as it starts

Resting on the floor needs to know how low the new pose reaches, so the program recomputes the kinematics and each body's lowest point before each rest. The push of a start in trouble comes last: it changes a velocity only, once the pose is settled.

reset_program
/// THE RESETS, once a decision after its judgement: every world whose episode ended begins another (`begin_reset`) at
/// its start frame's pose (`reset_pose`).
pub const reset_program = [_]Dispatch{
    // The decision's pushes, for the worlds still in their episode (`push_worlds`).
    .{ .kernel = "push_worlds", .invocations = .per_world },
    .{ .kernel = "begin_reset", .invocations = .per_world },
    .{ .kernel = "reset_pose", .invocations = .per_world_and_joint },
    // The new pose's kinematics and lowest points, then the rest on the floor (`rest_on_floor`); the observation
    // program that follows (`runResets`) runs the kinematics again for the rested pose.
    .{ .kernel = "kinematics_branch", .invocations = .per_world_and_branch },
    .{ .kernel = "body_lowest_points", .invocations = .per_world_and_body },
    .{ .kernel = "rest_on_floor", .invocations = .per_world },
    // The start noise (`perturb_start`), then the rest again after it (`rest_after_noise`), on the turned pose.
    .{ .kernel = "perturb_start", .invocations = .per_world_and_joint },
    .{ .kernel = "kinematics_branch", .invocations = .per_world_and_branch },
    .{ .kernel = "body_lowest_points", .invocations = .per_world_and_body },
    .{ .kernel = "rest_after_noise", .invocations = .per_world },
    // A start in trouble's push (`start_in_trouble`): a velocity only, after the pose is settled.
    .{ .kernel = "start_in_trouble", .invocations = .per_world },
};
runResets
/// THE RESETS on the twin (`reset_program`), after a decision's end: the decision's pushes, then every world whose
/// episode ended begins another - at the decision's clock (`DecisionClock`), taken here so it cannot be forgotten.
/// Then the observation program again - its kinematics and its observation - so a reset world's next action reads
/// its new episode's state, not its old one's last (worlds that did not reset recompute the same).
pub fn runResets(twin: CpuTwin, clock: DecisionClock) void {
    clock.applyTo(&g.P.options);
    twin.runProgram(&reset_program);
    twin.runProgram(&observe_program);
}

Where an episode starts. With the curriculum on, a start frame is not drawn evenly over the clip. The host keeps a probability for every bin of every clip, and before each decision it uploads them as running sums: for bin b, the total probability of bins 0 to b. begin_reset draws one number between 0 and 1, takes the first bin whose running sum exceeds it, and draws a frame inside that bin. A table of running sums turns a draw into a bin with comparisons alone, which is all a kernel needs.

bin                 0      1      2      3
probability         0.10   0.40   0.30   0.20
running sum         0.10   0.50   0.80   1.00

a draw of 0.63  ->  bin 2, the first whose running sum exceeds it

The clock of a decision. No draw on the device comes from a generator that remembers what it drew last. A draw is a function of numbers: the run's seed, the world, the episode's or the decision's number, what the draw is for, and an index. The same numbers give the same draw on the CPU and on any GPU, whatever the order the worlds are computed in. What changes from one decision to the next travels in one small value, the decision's clock: its number, the share of the push schedule opened by then, and the exploration's spread. Every program that draws takes the clock as an argument, so no draw can be made at an earlier decision's number.

DecisionClock
/// A DECISION's CLOCK, what changes from one decision to the next: its number (the counter of its draws - its pushes'
/// and its exploration's), the share of the push schedule opened by then (`Pushes.scheduleShare`), and the
/// exploration's spread, which training anneals. Every program that draws takes it as an argument (`runResets`,
/// `giveActions`), so no draw is made at a stale number.
pub const DecisionClock = struct {
    number: u32,
    push_schedule_share: f32,
    /// The exploration's spread (`explore_actions`): the noise scale times sigma, which training anneals.
    exploration_spread: f32 = 0,

    /// The clock written into the uniform.
    pub fn applyTo(clock: DecisionClock, options: *StepOptions) void {
        options.decision_number = clock.number;
        options.push_schedule_share = clock.push_schedule_share;
        options.exploration_spread = clock.exploration_spread;
    }
};

One call for the settings. The floor, the servo's law, the action's scale and filters, the limbs' stiffness, the episode's rules, the judgement's weights, the start's noise, the pushes and the curriculum are one value, TrackingSettings, written to a simulator by one call: Sim.setTracking on a device, CpuTwin.setTracking on the CPU twin. The call writes every setting it is given, so a setting is changed later by building the whole value again and calling again, and none is left at a default nobody chose. The filter applied at each decision is among them, decision_filter for the joints' offsets and decision_stiffness_filter for the limbs' stiffnesses. Left unsaid they are 1, no filter, so a trainer that filters names its shares each time it writes the settings.

The value is defined in robot.zig, below both the CPU tracker and the device's kernels, so a CPU fleet can describe its own task without knowing anything of a device: Fleet.trackingSettings builds the value from the fleet's options and task, and takes the trainer's filter shares as its argument. A device then mirrors a fleet in two calls:

settings = fleet.trackingSettings(the trainer's filter shares)
simulator.setTracking(settings)

The trainer and every check that compares a device with a fleet build the value through that one function, so what the checks compare is what a training run sets.

TrackingSettings
/// THE TRACKING TASK's SETTINGS, every world's, as ONE VALUE - what a CPU fleet's options and task say (robot_track's
/// `Fleet.trackingSettings` builds it), and what a simulator on a device takes in one call (robotgpu's
/// `CpuTwin.setTracking`, the host's `Sim.setTracking`), so no setting is left at a default nobody chose: the floor,
/// the servo's law and mode, the action's scale and filters, the limbs' stiffness, the head body, the episode's
/// rules, the judgement's weights, a start's rest and noise, the pushes and the curriculum. It lives here, below both
/// the fleet and the device's kernels, so the fleet can describe itself without importing the device, and one
/// definition serves the trainer and every check.
pub const TrackingSettings = struct {
    floor: Floor,
    law: ServoLaw,
    servo_mode: ServoMode,
    action_scale: f32,
    action_filter: f32,
    /// The limbs' stiffness share (the fleet's `stiffness_filter`).
    stiffness_filter: f32,
    /// The action filter at each decision (`filter_decision`): the trainer's `filter` and `stiffness_filter`. 1: no
    /// filter - the explored choice is the decision's action exactly.
    decision_filter: f32 = 1,
    decision_stiffness_filter: f32 = 1,
    limb_stiffness: Switch,
    head: ?usize,
    episode: EpisodeOptions,
    judgement: JudgementWeights,
    /// The run's draw seed and how a world's clip is chosen at a start (`begin_reset`).
    draw_seed: u32,
    clip_assignment: ClipAssignment,
    /// Whether a start rests on the floor, and its clearance (`rest_on_floor`).
    rest_on_floor: Switch,
    rest_clearance: f32,
    /// A start's noise, the fleet's `start_noise` (`perturb_start`).
    start_noise_pose: f32,
    start_noise_velocity: f32,
    /// The pushes (`push_worlds`, `start_in_trouble`): the fleet's `Pushes`, and a decision's seconds.
    push_every: u32,
    push_rate_per_second: f32,
    push_speed_min: f32,
    push_speed_max: f32,
    push_start_share: f32,
    decision_seconds: f32,
    /// SuperMimic's curriculum on the device (`begin_reset`), and its returns' discount.
    curriculum: Switch,
    curriculum_discount: f32,
    /// Each joint's rotation box, or null (the fleet's `rotation_boxes`): each ball's servo target clamped into its
    /// own.
    rotation_boxes: []const ?RotationBox,
};

One device for the physics and the learner

The simulator and the learner are separate compute pipelines, each with its own fields, but they can live on one device and one queue. A field's device buffer, with its size and the device it lives on, is available from its pipeline (deviceField), and one copy moves a range of bytes from a field of one pipeline into a field of another, recorded and submitted on their shared queue. A queue runs its submissions in order, so the copy follows every dispatch submitted before it. That is how experience goes from the physics to the learner without leaving the GPU: the simulator's observations, rewards and flags copied into the learner's tables after each decision.

copyFieldBetween
/// ONE COPY BETWEEN TWO PIPELINES ON ONE DEVICE: `byte_count` bytes from `source` at `source_byte` into `destination`
/// at `destination_byte`, recorded and submitted on their queue - after every dispatch submitted before it, since one
/// queue runs its submissions in order (call it after the source's work is submitted, not inside an open batch). How
/// experience goes from the physics to the learner without leaving the GPU. Both fields on one device and one queue;
/// WebGPU wants the offsets and the size in multiples of 4 bytes, and both ranges inside their buffers.
pub fn copyFieldBetween(
    source: DeviceField,
    source_byte: u32,
    destination: DeviceField,
    destination_byte: u32,
    byte_count: u32,
) void {
    const same_device: bool = source.device == destination.device and source.queue == destination.queue;
    assertf(same_device, @src(), "copyFieldBetween: the two fields live on different devices or queues", .{});
    const aligned: bool = source_byte % 4 == 0 and destination_byte % 4 == 0 and byte_count % 4 == 0;
    assertf(aligned, @src(), "copyFieldBetween: offsets and size must be multiples of 4 bytes", .{});
    const fits: bool = source_byte + byte_count <= source.bytes and destination_byte + byte_count <= destination.bytes;
    assertf(fits, @src(), "copyFieldBetween: {d} bytes overrun a field", .{byte_count});
    const encoder: wgpu.CommandEncoderHandle = wgpu.createCommandEncoder(source.device);
    wgpu.copyBufferToBuffer(encoder, source.buffer, source_byte, destination.buffer, destination_byte, byte_count);
    const commands: wgpu.CommandBufferHandle = wgpu.finishCommandEncoder(encoder);
    wgpu.queueSubmit(source.queue, commands);
}

Side by side

The page robotgpu_sidebyside shows the two engines at once. On the CPU, the tracking task's own fleet follows a dance with one character - Geno's task, his servo, floor and failure rule, the servo alone. On the GPU, the decision program follows the same dance with one world: the dance's frames on the device, each step's targets formed there, the frame advanced there; the host only submits a decision a frame, and reads the poses back a frame later for drawing alone. When the fleet begins a new episode - its failure rule, or the clip's end - the GPU's world begins it from exactly the fleet's new state. On the CPU twin the device's decision is the fleet's to the bit; on a GPU its arithmetic differs in the last bits - its division, square roots and fused multiply-adds round differently, and a stiff servo multiplies a target's last-bit difference by its stiffness - so the two move together and part by millimetres over seconds, while the servo itself follows the clip less closely than that.
